## 138. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/smart-objects/convert-smart-objects-to-layers.html

#### Learn how to unpack a Smart Object into its original layers in professional photoeditor so you can easily access and edit each one in the Layers panel.

You can convert an embedded or linked Smart Object back into its component layers in the document. If it contains multiple layers, photoeditor places them into a new group in the Layers panel. When you convert a multi-layer Smart Object in this way, photoeditor restores the original layers but discards any transforms or Smart Filters you have applied.

Select Window>Layers.

Select the Smart Object layer in the Layers panel.

Right-click the layer and select Convert To Layers.

![Image 1: The Layers panel is open, and a Smart Object layer is selected. A context menu is displayed with the Convert to Layers option highlighted.](https://helpx-prod.scene7.com/is/image/HelpxProd/The-Layers-panel-is-open--and-a-Smart-Object-layer?$pjpeg$&jpegSize=300&wid=1600)

Directly work with and modify each layer.

The Smart Object is converted, and its layers appear inside a folder in the Layers panel.

![Image 2: The Layers panel is open and a Smart Object is converted into a layer group. The original layers appear inside the group.](https://helpx-prod.scene7.com/is/image/HelpxProd/The-Layers-panel-is-open-and-a-Smart-Object-is-con?$pjpeg$&jpegSize=300&wid=1600)

Make the Smart Object editable by turning it into separate layers.

## 139. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/smart-objects/rasterize-smart-objects.html

#### Learn what happens when you turn a Smart Object into a regular layer in professional photoeditor and why it makes your edits permanent.

Rasterizing changes a Smart Object into a normal layer. Once rasterized, any transforms, warps, or filters are locked in and can't be adjusted later. Consider this option when you're sure you no longer need the flexibility of a Smart Object.

Select the Smart Object and choose Layer>Smart Objects>Rasterize to convert it to a regular layer with all edits fixed in place.

## 140. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/smart-objects/export-the-contents-of-an-embedded-smart-object.html

#### Learn how to save a Smart Object as a separate file in its original format so you can edit or reuse it outside professional photoeditor.

Select Window>Layers.

Select the Smart Object in the Layers panel.

Select Layer>Smart Objects>Export Contents.

Select a location on your computer and enter a file name.

Select Save to export the file.

photoeditor saves the Smart Object in its original format (JPEG, AI, TIFF, PDF, or others). If it was built from layers, it's saved as a PSB file.

## 141. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/smart-objects/reset-smart-object-transforms.html

Learn how to revert any transformations applied to a Smart Object in professional photoeditor, restoring it to its original state without affecting its contents.

Note

This feature works only with Smart Objects. To use it, first convert the layer to a Smart Object.

Select the Smart Object layer in the Layers panel.

Select Layer>Smart Objects>Reset Transform. The bounding box will reset to its original proportions and orientation, with any scaling, rotation, or skewing removed.

## 142. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/create-layer-compositions/align-content-of-layers-and-groups.html

Learn how to align multiple layers in professional photoeditor instantly to create sharp, consistent designs for professional layouts, grids, or UI mock-ups.

Select two or more layers (or layer groups) from the Layers panel.

Select Layer>Align, and select any of the following:

*   Top Edges icon: Aligns the top edge of the selected layers to the highest pixel of the group or selection border.
*   Vertical Centers icon: Aligns the vertical center pixel of the selected layers to the center of the group or selection border.
*   Bottom Edges icon: Aligns the bottom edge of the selected layers to the lowest pixel of the group or selection border.
*   Left Edges icon: Aligns the left pixel of the selected layers to the left pixel of the group or selection border.
*   Horizontal Centers icon: Aligns the horizontal center pixel of the selected layers to the horizontal center pixel of the group or selection border.
*   Right Edges icon: Aligns the right pixel of the selected layers to the right edge of the group selection border.

![Image 1: A sample document showing selected layers in the Layers panel and Align options in the Layer menu.](https://helpx-prod.scene7.com/is/image/HelpxProd/A-sample-document-showing-selected-layers-in-the-L?$pjpeg$&jpegSize=300&wid=1600)

Select multiple layers and select any align option from the Layer menu.


====================================================================================================

## 144. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/create-layer-compositions/distribute-layers-groups-evenly.html

Learn how to arrange layers evenly in professional photoeditor to streamline workflows for branding, print designs, or grid-based compositions.

Uneven spacing can make a design look cluttered and unprofessional. Distributing layers in photoeditor allows you to quickly arrange multiple objects, create visual harmony, and present clean and intentional designs. This method works best for structured workflows, like brochures, catalogs, or digital ads.

Select two or more layers (or groups of layers) from the Layers panel.

Select Layer>Distribute, and select any of the following:

*   Top Edges icon: Distributes the layers evenly based on their top edges.
*   Vertical Centers icon: Spaces the layers based on their vertical center points.
*   Bottom Edges icon: Distributes the layers evenly based on their bottom edges.
*   Left Edges icon: Distributes the layers evenly from their left edges.
*   Horizontal Centers icon: Spaces the layers based on their horizontal center points.
*   Right Edges icon: Distributes the layers evenly based on their right edges.
*   Horizontally icon: Spaces the horizontal spacing between the layers evenly.
*   Vertically icon: Spaces the vertical spacing between the layers evenly.

![Image 1: A sample document showing the selected layers in the Layers panel and the Distribute options in the Layer menu.](https://helpx-prod.scene7.com/is/image/HelpxProd/A-sample-document-showing-the-selected-layers-in-t?$pjpeg$&jpegSize=300&wid=1600)

Select the layers you want to distribute and select a distribute option from the Layer menu.


====================================================================================================

## 145. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/create-layer-compositions/create-and-manage-layer-comps.html

Capture multiple design variations in a single file using snapshots of layer visibility, position, and appearance.

Layer Comps let you present multiple design variations to clients without creating separate files for each version.

A layer comp is a snapshot of your Layers panel at a specific moment. Use Layer Comps when you need to show different layout options, color schemes, or content arrangements within a single document. Each comp can record layer visibility (showing or hiding layers), layer position, layer appearance (styles and blending modes), and Smart Object layer comp selections. Layer Comps work best for exploring design alternatives before finalizing your composition.

## Create a new layer comp

Select Window>Layer Comps to open the Layer Comps panel.

Arrange your layers to reflect the design state you want to capture.

Select the Create New Layer Comp button at the bottom of the Layer Comps panel.

Enter a descriptive name and optional comments in the New Layer Comp dialog box.

Select which layer properties to record: Visibility, Position, Appearance (Layer Style), or Layer Comp Selection For Smart Objects.

Select OK.

Your selected options become the default settings for future Layer Comps.

Duplicate an existing comp by dragging it to the Create New Layer Comp option in the Layer Comps panel.

## Apply and view layer comps

Open the Layer Comps panel by selecting Window>Layer Comps.

Select the Apply Layer Comp from Layer Comps context menu.

Use the Previous  and Next  controls at the bottom of the panel to cycle through all or specific comps.

Select the Apply Layer Comp option to restore your document.

## Update a layer comp

Select the layer comp to update in the Layer Comps panel.

Make your changes to layer visibility, position, or appearance in the Layers panel.

Select Layer Comp Options from the Layer Comps panel menu if you need to change which properties the comp records.

Select the Update Layer Comp button at the bottom of the Layer Comps panel.

## Use layer comps in Smart Objects

When you place a document containing layer comps as a [Smart Object](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/create-smart-objects.html) in another document, you can access those layer comps without opening the source file. This lets you switch between design variations at the Smart Object level.

Select the Smart Object layer in your document's Layers panel.

Open the Properties panel by selecting Window>Properties.

Select the layer comp you want to display from the Layer Comp menu in the Properties panel.

![Image 1: Layer comps menu in the properties panel in professional photoeditor diaplying list of layers comps with dedicated options.](https://helpx-prod.scene7.com/is/image/HelpxProd/Layer-comps-menu-in-the-properties-panel-in-professional-?$png$&jpegSize=100&wid=264)

The Smart Object updates to show the selected comp while preserving any transforms or effects you've applied to it.

## Clear layer comp warnings

A warning icon appears next to a layer comp when you delete, merge, or convert layers that the comp references. The comp can no longer be fully restored to its original state.

Select the warning icon next to the comp name to view the specific issue.

Select Clear in the message to remove the warning and preserve remaining layer properties.

Right-click the warning icon and select Clear Layer Comp Warning to clear a single warning or Clear All Layer Comp Warnings to clear all warnings at once.

You can also update the comp to its current state or ignore the warning if you don't need the missing layer information.

## Delete a layer comp

Select the layer comp in the Layer Comps panel.

Select the Delete icon at the bottom of the panel or drag the comp to the Delete icon.

Select Delete Layer Comp from the panel menu for an alternative method.

## Export layer comps to files

Select File>Export>Layer Comps To Files.

Choose your destination folder in the dialog box.

Select the file format for your exported comps.

Select Run to create individual files for each layer comp.

Each layer comp exports as a separate file with the comp name appended to the filename.


====================================================================================================

## 146. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/create-layer-compositions/create-collages-and-montages-using-layers.html

Add multiple images to a single document as separate layers using the Move tool.

Each image sits on its own layer, so you can adjust, move, or modify individual elements without affecting the rest of your collage.

Use this workflow when you want complete control over image placement and need flexibility to make changes throughout your creative process.

Before starting your collage, ensure you have:

*   Multiple images saved on your computer
*   A clear idea of your final collage dimensions
*   Basic familiarity with the Layers panel

Create a blank canvas, then add each image as a new layer. To display the Layers panel, select Window>Layers.

## Create the collage document and add images

Select File>New to create a blank document.

In the New Document dialog, specify your desired dimensions (such as 1920 x 1080 pixels or 8 x 10 inches), set resolution to 150 pixels/inch or higher, and choose a white background.

Select Create.

Select File>Open and navigate to your first source image.

Select the Move tool from the toolbar.

Click anywhere within the source image window, drag to your collage document window, and release to place the image as a new layer.

In the Layers panel, double-click the layer name (such as "Layer 1"), type a descriptive name for easy identification, and press Enter (Windows) or Return (macOS).

Close the source image window.

Repeat steps 4 through 7 for each additional image you want to add.

Source images with varying resolutions may appear at different sizes in your collage. You can adjust layer size later using Edit>Free Transform.

Each image now appears on its own named layer in the Layers panel. You can drag layers up or down to reorder them, adjust individual layer opacity, or apply transformations to refine your composition.

The Background layer remains beneath all added images, providing the base color you specified during document creation.

For more complex blending effects, explore layer blend modes by selecting different options from the blend mode dropdown at the top of the Layers panel. To merge your finished collage into a single layer, select Layer>Flatten Image, though this removes editing flexibility.


====================================================================================================

## 147. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/create-layer-compositions/create-layers-and-layer-groups.html

Learn how to use the Layers panel, Layer menu, and keyboard shortcuts to add new layers and organize content into groups in professional photoeditor on desktop.

Layers let you work on individual elements of your composition without affecting others, while layer groups help you organize related layers together for easier management. New layers appear above the currently selected layer in the Layers panel, or within a selected group.

Learn how to add content, isolate edits, or organize your document structure.

## Create a layer or group with default settings

Select Window>Layers.

In the Layers panel, select the layer above which you want to create the new layer or group.

Do one of the following:

*   Select Create a new layer at the bottom of the Layers panel. 
*   Select Create a new group at the bottom of the Layers panel. 

## Create a layer or group with custom options

Select Layer>New>Layer or Layer>New>Group.

You can also press Alt-click (Windows) or Option-click (macOS), then select Create a new layer or Create a new group.

In the New Layer or New Group dialog box, enter a name for the layer or group.

Configure these options:

Color**:** Assign a color label to help identify and organize the layer or group in the Layers panel.

Mode**:** Select how the new layer blends with the layers below.

Opacity**:** Set the transparency level of the new layer.

Use previous layer to create clipping mask**:**Clip the new layer so its contents are visible only where the layer below contains pixels.

To fill the layer with a neutral color for the selected blending mode, select Fill with neutral color.

Select OK.

Drag a file from Windows Explorer or macOS Finder into an open photoeditor document.

Use the on-canvas controls to move, scale, or rotate the imported content.

Press Enter (Windows) or Return (macOS) to commit the placement.

By default, photoeditor places imported files as [Smart Objects.](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/create-smart-objects.html) If you need a raster layer, select the imported Smart Object layer. Right-click the layer and select Rasterize Layer.

If you place a multilayer file, such as a PSD document, photoeditor places a flattened version on a single layer. To preserve individual layers, open the source file and duplicate the required layers into the destination document.

## Duplicate a layer with all effects

Select the layer you want to duplicate in the Layers panel.

Drag the layer to Create a new layer at the bottom-rightof the Layers panel.

## Convert a selection to a new layer

Use any selection tool to make a selection.

Select Layer>New>Layer Via Copy to copy the selection to a new layer or Layer Via Cut to move the selection to a new layer.

Group existing layers  
Create groups to apply adjustments, masks, or transformations to multiple layers simultaneously. You can also nest groups within other groups to create a hierarchical organization for complex documents.

In the Layers panel, select multiple layers by pressing Ctrl-click (Windows) or Command-click (macOS) for each layer or select Layer>Group Layers to create a group.

Double-click the group name and enter a name.

Mode determines the [blending mode](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/blending-modes.html) for how the layer interacts with the layers under it. If the selected blend mode requires pixel content, select Fill with neutral color.

## 148. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/create-layer-compositions/non-destructive-editing-techniques.html

Find essential methods for preserving original image data while making edits in professional photoeditor on desktop.

Non-destructive editing preserves original pixel data, allowing you to revise decisions without degrading image quality through multiple edit cycles.

Non-destructive editing means making changes to an image without permanently altering the source pixel data. This approach maintains flexibility throughout your workflow, letting you experiment freely while keeping the option to revert to the original at any time.

### Adjustment layers

Apply color and tonal corrections without permanently changing pixel values. Adjustment layers are placed on top of image layers and remain fully editable. You can modify settings, hide, or remove them at any time without affecting the underlying image data.

### Smart Objects

Preserve source content from raster or vector files, enabling lossless scaling, rotation, warping, and transformation. Smart Objects maintain a reference to the original data, so repeated transformations never degrade quality.

### Smart Filters

Filters applied to Smart Objects become Smart Filters, which remain editable and can be masked, adjusted, or removed without affecting the underlying image. The Shadow/Highlight command can be applied as a Smart Filter for reversible tonal adjustments.

### Layer-based retouching

Tools like Clone Stamp, Healing Brush, Spot Healing Brush, Content-Aware Patch, and Content-Aware Move support retouching on separate layers. Select Sample All Layers from the Options bar, then Ignore Adjustment Layers to prevent adjustment layers from affecting the retouch layer twice. This approach lets you discard or refine retouching work independently.

### Camera Raw editing

Camera Raw applies adjustments to RAW, JPEG, or TIFF images while storing settings separately from the original files. This preserves source data for batch processing and future revisions. Opening Camera Raw files as Smart Objects in photoeditor lets you reconfigure Camera Raw settings even after additional editing.

### Non-destructive cropping

After creating a cropping rectangle with the Crop tool, leave Delete Cropped Pixels unchecked in the options bar to preserve hidden areas. Restore cropped content by selecting Image>Reveal All or by dragging the Crop tool beyond the image edge.

This option is unavailable for images containing only a Background layer. Convert the Background to a regular layer first.

### Masking

Layer masks and vector masks hide pixels without deleting them, making all masking decisions reversible. Filter masks control which areas of a Smart Object layer show Smart Filter effects.

## Technique comparison

## Combining techniques

Most professional workflows combine multiple non-destructive methods. For example, you might convert a layer to a Smart Object, apply Smart Filters for effects, add adjustment layers for color correction, and use layer masks to control where each effect appears. This layered approach provides maximum flexibility while keeping file size manageable.


====================================================================================================

## 149. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/create-layer-compositions/create-and-process-image-stacks.html

Use Smart Objects to combine multiple images and reduce noise or remove unwanted objects in professional photoeditor on desktop.

Image stacks give precise control over how multiple exposures combine into a single enhanced result.

An image stack combines multiple images with similar framing but different qualities into a Smart Object. You can then apply stack modes processing options that analyze corresponding pixels across all layers to create a composite that eliminates noise, distortion, or unwanted objects. Image stacks work best with images of identical dimensions taken from a fixed viewpoint, such as still photos from a tripod or frames from a stationary video camera.

You can create image stacks manually for full control over layer alignment and mode selection, or use the Statistics script to automate the entire workflow.

## Combine images into one multi-layered document

Select File>Scripts>Load Files into Stack, or manually copy each image as a new layer.

If your Background layer is included, convert it to a regular layer by right-clicking it and selecting Layer From Background.

Select Select>All Layers to select every layer in the document.

Select Edit>Auto-Align Layers, then Auto as the alignment option, and select OK.

With all layers still selected, select Layer>Smart Objects>Convert to Smart Object.

Select Layer>Smart Objects>Stack Mode and choose a rendering mode such as Mean for noise reduction or Median for object removal.

Stack mode rendering is non-destructive. You can change modes at any time to produce different effects while preserving the original image data. Common uses include reducing noise in forensic, medical, or astrophotographic images, and removing transient elements like pedestrians or vehicles from architectural photography. The rendered composite appears immediately.

## Create and render a stack using the Statistics script

Select File>Scripts>Statistics.

Choose a stack mode from the Choose Stack Mode menu, then specify your source images by selecting Browse to choose files or a folder.

Select Attempt To Automatically Align Source Images if your images need registration.

Select OK. photoeditor combines the images into a Smart Object and applies the selected stack mode automatically.

The Statistics script completes the entire stack creation and rendering workflow in a single operation.

## Edit the contents of an image stack

Select the Smart Object layer containing the stack, then Layer>Smart Objects>Edit Contents.

Make your changes to individual layers within the stack document, then save and close the file to update the rendered composite.

## Remove stack rendering

Select the Smart Object layer, then select Layer>Smart Objects>Stack Mode>None.

Removing the stack mode leaves the Smart Object intact but disables composite rendering, revealing only the top layer.

## Convert a stack to a regular layer

Select Layer>Smart Objects>Rasterize to permanently apply the current stack rendering and convert the Smart Object to a standard layer.

Duplicate the Smart Object layer before rasterizing to preserve the ability to re-render the stack with different modes later.

## 150. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/layout-design-tools/get-started-artboards.html

Learn about artboards in professional photoeditor, their structure, benefits, and customization for multi-screen design.

## Overview

Artboards are special layer groups that clip the contents of any contained elements to their boundaries. Web and UX designers can use artboards to create designs for multiple devices and screen sizes simultaneously.

While creating artboards, you can choose from various preset sizes or define your own custom artboard size. Artboards are useful even if you design just one screen size. For example, while designing a website, you can use artboards to view designs for different pages side-by-side and in context.

## Structure and organization

*   Artboards clip the contents of the contained elements. 
*   Artboards can include layers and layer groups, but not other artboards. They act as individual canvases within a document. 
*   In the Layers panel, elements within an artboard appear alongside other layers and layer groups. 
*   Any layers outside an artboard are grouped separately in the Layers panel and remain unclipped.

## Benefits of artboards

*   **Side-by-side designs:**View multiple versions in one file for better screen consistency.
*   **Organized layers:**Layers within artboards are grouped and clipped automatically.
*   **Flexible structure:**Artboards hold layers and groups, but not other artboards.

## Artboard customization

You can customize the visual appearance of your artboards to suit your workflow better.

Select Edit>Preferences>Interface >Appearance>Artboards to access these settings.

## 159. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/layout-design-tools/apply-patterns-to-template-images.html

Use Pattern Fill layers and clipping masks to add repeating designs non-destructively to mockup templates.

Pattern Fill layers let you showcase custom patterns on product mockups, apparel templates, and packaging designs while keeping all edits fully adjustable.

This workflow applies patterns to template images using Pattern Fill layers and clipping masks. Pattern Fill layers keep your pattern editable—you can change the pattern, scale, or angle at any time without affecting the original template. This approach works with any template that includes designated layers for customization, such as apparel mockups, product packaging, or surface designs.

Before applying patterns to templates, ensure you have patterns available in your Patterns panel and a template file with clearly labeled layers for customization.

*   Patterns saved in the Patterns panel
*   A template document with target layers for pattern application
*   photoeditor 2021 or later for full Pattern Fill layer support

## Add a Pattern Fill layer

Open your template document and locate the layer where you want to apply the pattern in the Layers panel.

Select the target layer, then select Layer>New Fill Layer>Pattern.

Enter a descriptive name for the layer in the New Layer dialog and select OK.

In the Pattern Fill dialog, select a pattern from the pattern picker dropdown menu.

Adjust the Scale slider to resize the pattern and set the Angle to rotate it if needed.

Select OK to create the Pattern Fill layer.

The pattern now fills the entire canvas on a new layer above your target layer. To restrict the pattern to only the target layer's visible areas, create a clipping mask.

## Create a clipping mask

Hold Alt(Windows) or Option(macOS) and position your cursor over the dividing line between the Pattern Fill layer and the layer directly below it in the Layers panel.

When the cursor changes to a clipping icon, select to create the clipping mask.

The pattern now appears only where the layer beneath contains pixels. You can also create a clipping mask by selecting the Pattern Fill layer and choosing Layer>Create Clipping Mask, or by right-clicking the Pattern Fill layer and selecting Create Clipping Mask.

To modify your pattern after applying it, double-click the Pattern Fill layer thumbnail to reopen the Pattern Fill dialog. From here, you can select a different pattern, adjust the scale, or change the angle. You can also experiment with blend modes in the Layers panel—Multiply, Overlay, and Soft Light, for example—changing how the pattern interacts with the layer beneath.

Some templates include multiple layers for different design areas. Create separate Pattern Fill layers for each area to maintain independent control over each pattern application.

For templates with complex layer structures, such as apparel mockups, look for layer groups labeled "graphic," "design," or "custom." Hide any placeholder layers within these groups before adding your Pattern Fill layer to avoid visual conflicts.


====================================================================================================

## 160. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/printed-image-resolution.html

Learn how image dimensions and resolution affect the quality of printed output.

Printed image resolution refers to the number of pixels assigned to each inch of an image when it’s printed, measured in pixels per inch (ppi). The more pixels per inch, the higher the resolution, and a high-resolution image will generally produce sharper and more detailed printed results.

Image dimensions represent the total number of pixels along the width and height of an image. While dimensions determine the pixel count, resolution determines how densely those pixels are packed when sent to print.

When adjusting image dimensions or resolution, the total image data remains constant unless the image is resampled. If you increase resolution, the width and height will decrease proportionally to preserve the same amount of data, and lowering resolution will increase the image’s physical size when printed. Resampling is required to change the number of pixels in the image itself.


====================================================================================================

*   Resolution: Specifies the number of pixels per inch (ppi) for print quality.

## 163. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/monitor-resolution-and-image-display-size.html

Learn how monitor resolution and image dimensions affect the size at which images appear on screen.

Monitor resolution is measured in pixels. If the resolution of the monitor matches the pixel dimensions of an image, the image will fill the screen when viewed at 100%.

## Factors that determine image size on screen

The size at which an image appears depends on:

*   Pixel dimensions of the image
*   Size and resolution settings of the monitor

In photoeditor, you can adjust the onscreen magnification to comfortably work with images of any pixel dimensions, regardless of the monitor size.

## Practical consideration

When preparing images for onscreen viewing, always account for the lowest monitor resolution on which your photo is likely to be displayed. This ensures that the content is visible without requiring unnecessary scrolling or resizing.


====================================================================================================

## 164. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/file-size.html

Learn how professional photoeditor pixel dimensions, format, and resolution impact image file size.

The file size of an image refers to its digital storage size, measured in kilobytes (KB), megabytes (MB), or gigabytes (GB). File size is directly proportional to the pixel dimensions of an image. Larger pixel dimensions allow for greater detail at a given print size but require more disk space and can slow down editing or printing. Therefore, balancing resolution and file size is essential to achieving quality and efficiency.

## Factors affecting file size

*   **Pixel dimensions**: More pixels mean larger files.
*   **File format**: Compression methods vary across GIF, JPEG, PNG, and TIFF, resulting in different file sizes for the exact image dimensions.
*   **Color depth and layers**: Higher bit-depth, multiple layers, and additional channels increase file size.

## photoeditor limitations

photoeditor supports images up to 300,000 by 300,000 pixels. This limit defines the maximum print size and resolution possible within the application.


====================================================================================================

## 165. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/printer-resolution.html

Learn how printer resolution, image resolution, and screen frequency work together to determine print quality.

Printer resolution is measured in dpi—a higher dpi value results in finer printed output. Most inkjet printers operate in the range of 720 to 2880 dpi. While printer resolution affects the sharpness of printed details, it isn't the same as image resolution. An image resolution of at least 220 pixels per inch (ppi) is usually sufficient to achieve high-quality photo prints on an inkjet printer.

## Screen frequency

Screen frequency refers to the number of printer dots or halftone cells per inch to print grayscale images or color separations. Also called screen ruling or line screen, it’s measured in lines per inch (lpi). Higher output device resolutions allow for finer screen ruling, improving printed images' detail and tonal quality.

## Relationship between image resolution and screen frequency

The balance between image resolution and screen frequency plays a key role in print quality. The image resolution should generally be 1.5 to 2 times the screen frequency for the best halftone results. Lower resolutions may still provide acceptable results in some cases, depending on the image and the printer being used.

## Additional considerations

To determine your printer’s screen frequency, refer to its documentation or consult your print service provider. Some devices, such as image setters or 600-dpi laser printers, may use screening methods other than halftoning. For these printers, check the documentation or seek guidance on the recommended image resolutions for optimal output.


====================================================================================================

## 166. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/resolution-specs-for-printing-images.html

Learn how print resolution, default printer settings, and viewing distance affect image quality.

## Standard print resolution

A resolution of 300 pixels per inch (ppi) is considered the industry standard for producing high-quality prints. Images appear sharp and detailed at this resolution, especially when viewed up close. While 300 ppi is ideal for smaller prints, lower resolutions can still work well for large-format prints meant to be viewed from a distance. For example, billboards can be printed at lower resolutions without sacrificing visual impact because fine detail isn't noticeable from afar.

## Default printer resolution

Most printers default to a resolution of 300 ppi. If you attempt to print an image with a lower resolution, the printer will automatically adjust the settings to maintain its default resolution. As a result, you cannot print below the printer’s set resolution. If the image isn't resized before printing, the printer may enlarge it, affecting clarity.

## Viewing print size on screen

In photoeditor, preview an image's printed size by selecting **View > Print Size**. The image will then be displayed at its approximate printed size, though the preview may vary depending on your monitor’s size and resolution.

## 167. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/preserve-visual-content-when-scaling-images.html

Learn how to use Content-Aware Scale in photoeditor to resize images while protecting important details and proportions.

Select Select**>**All.

Perform this step only if you’re scaling a Background layer.

Select Edit**>**Content-Aware Scale.

In the Options bar, set any of the following:

*   Reference Point Location**:** Select a square to fix the scaling point (default is center).
*   Use Relative Positioning for Reference Point**:** Reposition the point relative to its current placement.
*   Reference Point Position**:** Enter exact X and Y pixel values for the reference point.
*   Scaling Percentage**:** Define width (W) and height (H) percentages. Select **Maintain Aspect Ratio** to keep proportions.
*   Amount**:** Control the ratio of content-aware scaling to normal scaling.
*   Protect**:** Select an alpha channel to protect an area.
*   Protect Skin Tones**:** Preserve regions with skin tones.

Drag a handle on the bounding box to scale the image. Hold Shift while dragging a corner handle to scale proportionately.

Select Commit Transform to apply or Cancel Transform to discard.

## 174. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/resizing-parameters-in-photoeditor.html

Learn about resizing parameters in professional photoeditor, including dimensions, resolution, and resampling, to adjust image size with quality.

The **Image Size** dialog provides controls for adjusting image size and resolution. These parameters let you define dimensions, units, proportions, and resampling behavior.

## Dimensions

**Parameter****Description****Units**
**Width**The horizontal measurement of the image.Pixels, inches, cm, mm, points, picas,
**Height**The vertical measurement of the image.Pixels, inches, cm, mm, points, picas,
**Resolution**The pixel density of the image.Pixels per inch (PPI) or pixels per centimeter (PPC)

## Constrain proportions

*   **On (default):** Maintains the aspect ratio between width and height.
*   **Off:** Allows independent adjustment of width and height, which may distort the image.

## Resolution

Resolution determines how many pixels appear per inch or centimeter, affecting print and display quality.

**Resolution range****Use**
72–96 PPI Web and screen display
150–300 PPI Standard printing
300+ PPI High-quality printing and detailed images

## 175. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/change-the-canvas-size.html

Add or remove workspace around your image without affecting the image itself in professional photoeditor on desktop.

Use the Canvas Size command when you need to create borders, add room for design elements, or adjust your composition area. This changes the editable workspace while preserving your image's pixel count.

To change image dimensions or resolution, learn about [Resize images](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/resize-images.html). To remove portions of an image, use the Crop tool.

## Adjust canvas dimensions

Select Image>Canvas Size.

In the Canvas Size dialog, enter new dimensions in the Width and Height fields, then choose your unit of measurement.

To add or subtract space instead of setting absolute dimensions, select Relative to current dimension and enter values. Use positive values to increase the canvas size or negative values to decrease it.

Select a square in the Anchor grid to position your image within the new canvas. The selected square shows where your image stays fixed.

Choose an option from the Canvas Extension Color menu to fill the added canvas area.

Select OK.

## Canvas extension color options

When you increase the canvas size, photoeditor fills the new space based on your selection:

*   Foreground**:** Uses the current foreground color from the Tools panel.
*   Background**:** Uses the current background color from the Tools panel.
*   White, Black, or Gray**:** Uses the specified neutral color.
*   Other**:** Opens the Color Picker to select a custom color.

You can also select the color swatch next to the Canvas extension color menu to open the Color Picker directly. The Canvas extension color menu appears only when your document contains a Background layer. Documents with transparent layers show transparency in added canvas areas.

## Create a photo frame using Actions

Select Window>Actions.

Select the Actions panel menu icon and then choose Frames.

Select one of the frame actions, such as Wood Frame or Photo Corners.

Select the Play Selection button at the bottom of the Actions panel to apply the frame.

The frame action automatically increases your canvas size and applies styled borders around your image. For best results, work on a duplicate of your original image. Use [Automate tasks with actions](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/actions-actions-panel.html) to learn how to record and use actions.

## 176. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/image-size-resolution-and-resampling.html

Understand how pixel dimensions, resolution, and physical size work together to control image quality and output in professional photoeditor on desktop.

Pixel dimensions, resolution, and physical size are three connected properties that images have that adjust together to maintain their mathematical relationship.

Understanding how these properties interact helps you make better choices when preparing images for screen or print. You can control how resizing affects image quality and how to preserve its quality.

## Dimensions of image size

Every digital image has three parameters:

*   **Pixel dimensions:** Defines the total number of pixels in your image, expressed as width × height. A 3000 × 2000 pixel image contains 6 million individual pixels. This is the actual data in your image file.
*   **Resolution:** Specifies how densely pixels are packed when the image is printed or displayed. It's measured in pixels per inch (ppi) or pixels per centimeter. Resolution doesn't change the image data but only determines how that data is translated to physical measurements.
*   **Physical size:** Indicates the dimensions of the printed image in inches or centimeters. Physical size matters for print output. Screen display depends on pixel dimensions and the monitor’s resolution.

## Relationship between the parameters

photoeditor calculates these three values in the following manner:

*   Physical size = pixel dimensions ÷ resolution
*   Resolution = pixel dimensions ÷ physical size
*   Pixel dimensions = physical size × resolution

You can't set all three values independently. When you change one parameter, at least one other must adjust to maintain this relationship. The Image Size dialog provides two distinct modes for managing these adjustments: resizing without resampling and resizing with resampling.

## Resizing without resampling

When you resize without resampling, you keep the exact pixel dimensions of your image. The total amount of data remains constant. No pixels are added or removed, and only the relationship between resolution and physical size changes.

If you increase the physical print size, the resolution decreases proportionally. The same pixels are spread across a larger area, creating lower pixel density.

This approach works well when you need to adjust print dimensions or resolution while preserving every pixel of your original image data.

**Example**: A 400 × 400 pixel image at 100 ppi prints at 4 × 4 inches. Change the physical size to 2 × 2 inches without resampling, and photoeditor automatically adjusts resolution to 200 ppi—the same 400 × 400 pixels now fit into half the space.

## Resampling: Changing pixel dimensions

Resampling adds or removes pixels from your image. It changes the amount of data in your file. Enable resampling in the Image Size dialog to recalculate pixel information to match your target dimensions.

## Upsampling

Upsampling adds pixels when you increase image dimensions. photoeditor estimates what new pixels should look like, which can reduce sharpness and introduce artifacts.

Modern algorithms produce better results than older methods, but upsampling can’t add detail that wasn't in the original image.

## Downsampling

Downsampling removes pixels when you decrease dimensions. photoeditor combines multiple pixels into a single pixel, discarding information in the process. This works better than upsampling because real image data informs the calculations. Downsampling reduces quality.

When you change any value in the Image Size dialog with resampling enabled, pixel dimensions adjust while maintaining your specified resolution and physical size. This differs from resizing without resampling, where pixel dimensions remain fixed.

## Screen versus print size

Images behave differently on screen than in print because monitors and printers use different display methods.

## On screen

Your monitor resolution (the total number of pixels your screen can display) determines how large images appear. A 1920 × 1080 pixel monitor displays exactly that many pixels across its physical dimensions.

When you view a 960 × 540 pixel image at 100% magnification, it occupies one-quarter of the screen. The same image on a 3840 × 2160 pixel monitor occupies one-sixteenth of the screen area. It appears physically smaller despite having the same pixel dimensions.

Your image's resolution setting doesn't affect how it's displayed on the screen. A 1000 × 1000 pixel image looks the same on screen whether its resolution is set to 72 ppi or 300 ppi. Both contain the same pixel data.

## In print

Resolution determines physical size in print. A 1000 × 1000 pixel image prints at approximately 13.9 × 13.9 inches at 72 ppi, but only 3.3 × 3.3 inches at 300 ppi.

Higher resolution produces smaller prints with greater detail. Lower resolution creates larger prints with less detail per inch.

Professional printing typically needs 300 ppi for the best quality. Screen-destined images work well at 72–96 ppi.

## Working with the Image Size dialog

The Image Size dialog lets you control these parameters.

At the top, you see the current file size and pixel dimensions, which is the actual data in your image. Below that, you can adjust width, height, and resolution using various measurement units.

The Resample checkbox determines your working mode:

*   When deselected, you can only modify resolution and physical size. Pixel dimensions stay locked, preserving all original image data.
*   When selected, you can change any value, and photoeditor recalculates pixel dimensions as needed.

The Constrain Aspect Ratio link  icon determines whether width and height change proportionally or independently. Linked values maintain your image's original proportions. Unlinked values let you stretch or compress the image, which typically distorts the content.

## Practical implications

Understanding these relationships helps you prepare images effectively.

For screen display, focus on pixel dimensions and ignore resolution settings.

For print, set resolution appropriately for your output device (typically 300 ppi). Let physical size adjust based on available pixels or enable resampling if you need specific print dimensions.

Avoid resampling when possible, especially upsampling. Start with properly sized source images rather than enlarging small files. When you must resample, do it only once, as repeated resampling compounds the quality loss.

For significant size increases, check whether your output truly needs all those pixels. Consider whether a smaller dimension serves your needs.

Resize images provides step-by-step instructions for [resizing images.](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/resize-images.html) Use [Crop tool](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/kb/advanced-cropping-resizing-resampling-photoeditor.html) for details on changing image dimensions.

## 177. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/resize-adjust-resolution/transform-images-shapes-and-paths.html

Learn how to apply scaling, rotation, skewing, distortion, perspective, and warp transformations in a single continuous operation in professional photoeditor on desktop.

Free Transform combines multiple transformation types into a single operation. Use it to resize, rotate, or reshape pixel-based layers, vector shapes, paths, and selections without switching between separate commands.

For advanced content-aware resizing, use Content-Aware Scale instead. For complex mesh-based deformations, use the Puppet Warp tool.

By default, when you drag a corner handle, photoeditor scales proportionally. The Maintain aspect ratio icon is ON in the Options bar. Select Shift while transforming to toggle between proportional and non-proportional scaling. photoeditor remembers your last scaling behavior setting and applies it when you launch the application.

## Enter Free Transform mode

Alternatively, select Show Transform Controls in the Options bar when using the Move tool for pixel-based content or the Path Selection tool for vector content. This displays the transform bounding box whenever you select a transformable element.

## Apply transformations

## Adjust the reference point.

All scaling, rotation, and other transformations occur relative to this reference point. Moving it to a corner causes rotations to pivot around that corner instead of the center.

## Apply warp transformations

## Commit or cancel the transformation

When transforming bitmap content, the image becomes slightly less sharp each time you commit a transformation. Apply multiple transformations in a single operation before committing to minimize quality loss. Smart Objects preserve image quality through repeated transformations.

To revert to the legacy transform behavior where dragging a corner handle scales non-proportionally by default, select Edit>Preferences>General (Windows) or photoeditor>Preferences>General (macOS), then turn on Legacy Free Transform.

To correct perspective distortion while cropping, use the [Perspective Crop tool](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/crop-straighten/transform-perspective-while-cropping.html).

Original Ratio Maintains the image’s original aspect ratio

2 : 3 (4 : 6)Crops to a standard 4 × 6 print ratio

## 184. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/transform-manipulate-reshape/transformation-options-in-professional-photoeditor.html

Learn about Transform commands in professional photoeditor to resize, reshape, and adjust perspective on layers, selections, masks, and shapes.

You can find these commands under Edit>Transform to adjust layout, refine composition, or reshape content creatively.

**Transformation****Function**
Scale Resizes the selected content horizontally, vertically, or both.
Rotate Rotates the selection around a reference point.
Skew Slants the content vertically or horizontally by moving its sides.
Distort Stretches or compresses content freely by moving individual corners or edges.
Perspective Applies perspective by moving one corner, causing the opposite corner to move symmetrically.
Warp Bends and reshapes content using a customizable grid or preset warp styles.
Rotate 180°Rotates the object by 180 degrees.
Rotate 90° Clockwise Rotates the object 90 degrees clockwise.
Rotate 90° Counter Clockwise Rotates the object 90 degrees counterclockwise.
Flip Horizontal Flips the selection along the vertical axis.
Flip Vertical Flips the selection along the horizontal axis.

*   Rotate 90° Clockwise: Rotates the image 90 degrees to the right.
*   Rotate 90° Counter Clockwise: Rotates the image 90 degrees to the left.
*   Flip Horizontal: Mirrors the image from left to right.
*   Flip Vertical: Flips the image upside down along the vertical axis.

## 188. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/transform-manipulate-reshape/apply-transformations.html

Learn how to use Transform in professional photoeditor to scale, rotate, skew, distort, add perspective, or warp layers or selections.

Transformations help you change the appearance of selected objects, such as resizing, rotating, or altering perspective. photoeditor offers flexible tools to apply these modifications directly to layers, text, shapes, or selections.

## Transform an object or layer

You can only transform the active layer or selection. To transform a background layer, first convert it into a regular layer by selecting the lock  icon next to it in the Layers panel.

Select the layer or area you want to transform.

Go to Edit>Transform, then select a transformation type:

*   Scale to resize
*   Rotate to turn
*   Skew to slant
*   Distort to stretch
*   Perspective to shift depth
*   Warp to bend and curve freely

Press Enter (Windows), Return (macOS), or select Done to confirm the transformation.

## Repeat a transformation

You must apply at least one transformation using Transform or Free Transform before Again becomes available.

Select the layer or object you want to transform again.

Select Edit>Transform>Again from the menu bar.

Transformations start from the object's center by default, but you can move the reference point to control the direction or origin. The reference point is hidden by default and must be made visible.

Select the layer, selection, or object you want to transform.

Select Edit>Transform and select a transformation type, or select Edit>Free Transform to apply multiple transformations.

In the Options bar, select the Reference point location icon to display the reference point on the document.

Drag the reference point to a new position inside or outside the bounding box to set a new transformation origin.

## 190. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/transform-manipulate-reshape/rotate-objects.html

Rotate a selected layer on the canvas to view it from different angles and adjust its orientation.

## Apply rotation

Select File>Open to select and open an image.

Select a pixel layer or Smart Object layer with the object you want to rotate.

Rasterize Shape and Type layers before using Rotate Object.

Select Transform image>Rotate object from the Contextual Task Bar or Edit>Rotate Object.

Drag on the canvas to rotate the object, use the on-canvas controls to adjust the rotation, or select Rotate or Tilt from the Contextual Task Bar. You can also refine the rotation in the Properties panel.

Select Done to apply the rotation.

A low-resolution preview appears while rotating the object. After you select Done, the result is rendered at the full image resolution. You can reopen Rotate Object to make further adjustments.

## Edit rotation

Select the rotated layer, then Transform image>Rotate object from the Contextual Task Bar or Edit>Rotate Object.

Drag the on-canvas rotation controls to adjust the object, or use Rotate or Tilt from the Contextual Task Bar. For precise adjustments, enter rotation values in the Properties panel.

Select Done to apply the updated rotation.

## 191. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/crop-resize-transform/transform-manipulate-reshape/combine-images-in-photoeditor.html

Learn how to use layers, object selection, and masking to blend multiple photos into a unified composition in professional photoeditor on desktop.

Use object selection, layer masks, and edge refinement to combine image elements. For panoramic stitching, use File>Automate>Photomerge instead, and for focus stacking, select Auto-Blend Layers with Stack Images.

## Prepare your layers

In the Layers panel (select Window>Layers if not visible), locate the layer containing the element you want to add to your composition.

Select the visibility icon (eye) next to the layer to make it visible if it's currently hidden.

Select the layer name to make it active.

## Select your subject

Select Object Selection tool from the toolbar or press W.

Hover over the element you want to select until a pink highlight appears around it.

Select the highlighted area to create a selection.

To select multiple objects on the same layer, hold Shift and select additional elements. The Object Selection tool works best with images that have clear contrast between subjects and backgrounds.

## Refine the selection edges

With your selection active, select Select and Mask from the Options bar.

In the Properties panel, choose a View Mode that clearly shows the edges of your selected element.

Select the Refine Edge Brush tool from the Select and Mask toolbar.

Brush along the outer edges of your selection to clean up details like hair, fur, or complex shapes.

Use the[and]keys to adjust brush size as you work.

In the Output Settings section at the bottom of the Properties panel, set Output To to Layer Mask.

Select OK to apply the mask.

The refined selection appears on its own layer with a layer mask that isolates the selected element from its background.

## Position and arrange elements

Select the Move tool from the toolbar or press V.

Drag the masked element to position it where you want it in the composition.

In the Layers panel, drag layers up or down to reorder them and control which elements appear in front of or behind others.

Make remaining layers visible by selecting their visibility icons to reveal the complete composition.

To resize or transform an element, select Ctrl+T(Windows) or Command+T(macOS) to access Free Transform. Hold Shift while dragging a corner handle to maintain the original proportions.

## Refine your composite

Once positioned, refine your composite:

Adjust individual layer opacity in the Layers panel.

Experiment with blend modes from the blend mode dropdown menu.

Add adjustment layers from the Adjustments panel to unify lighting and color.

To move an element between documents, select the Move tool and drag a layer from one image window into another. Hold Shift while dragging to center the element in the destination image.


====================================================================================================

## 192. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/make-selections/get-started-selections/selection-tools-overview.html

Learn about the different selection tools available in professional photoeditor.

A moving dashed border appears around active selections. When you remove a selection, the border disappears, and all pixels become available for editing. Select Select>Deselect, or press Ctrl + D (Windows) or Command + D (macOS).

If you are using the Rectangular Marquee**tool**, Elliptical Marquee**tool**, or Lasso**tool**, select anywhere outside the selected area to deselect.


====================================================================================================

## 193. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/make-selections/get-started-selections/select-objects-with-object-selection-tool.html

Learn how to select specific objects or areas in your image using the Object Selection tool.

Using the Object Selection tool, draw a rectangle or lasso around the area, or let it automatically detect and select the object or region in the image.

Select the Object Selection**tool** from the toolbar.

Hover over the image to automatically highlight the object or region you want to select.

![Image 1: Object finder is highlighted in the settings menu of the Object Selection tool in the Options bar, above an image of buildings on the photoeditor canvas.](https://helpx-prod.scene7.com/is/image/HelpxProd/Object-finder-is-highlighted-in-the-settings-menu-?$pjpeg$&jpegSize=300&wid=1500)

Select Set additional options to disable Object finder from the Option bar to manually define a region using either Rectangle or Lasso tool.

Select a selection mode from the Options bar to modify further:

*   New selection: Create a selection when nothing is currently selected.
*   Add to selection: Include additional areas by hovering over or drawing around them.
*   Subtract from selection: Exclude unwanted areas by hovering over or drawing around them.
*   Intersect with selection: Retain only the overlapping areas of multiple selections.

Refine the selection using additional options from the Options bar:

*   Sample All Layers: Include all visible layers in the selection.
*   Hard Edge: Apply sharp edges to the selection boundary.


====================================================================================================

## 194. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/make-selections/get-started-selections/isolate-specific-areas-in-images-with-lasso-tool.html

Learn how to use brush gestures, freehand drawing, or edge-snapping techniques to isolate specific image areas for editing.

Lasso tools let you define selection borders by drawing directly on the canvas. Use these tools when you need flexible, hand-drawn selections that follow irregular shapes or defined edges. For geometric selections, use the [Marquee tools](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/selecting-marquee-tools.html). For quick object isolation, try the [Object Selection tool](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/tool-techniques/object-selection-tool.html).

All lasso tools share common selection mode options in the options bar: New Selection, Add to Selection, Subtract from Selection, and Intersect with Selection. You can also set Feather values to soften selection edges and enable Anti-alias for smoother boundaries.

Practice these selection techniques in your own images to master each lasso tool's unique approach.

## Select with the Selection Brush tool

Select the Selection Brush tool from the Lasso tool group in the toolbar.

Brush or draw a lasso gesture around the area you want to select—a colored overlay appears over your selection.

Adjust Hardness and Opacity in the options bar to control edge softness and selection transparency.

Toggle between Add and Subtract modes in the options bar to refine the selection.

Select the gear icon in the options bar and choose a different color from the Colors dropdown to change the overlay color if needed.

Note

The Selection Brush displays a colored overlay instead of marching ants. Switch to another selection tool to see the marching ant's border.

## Select with the Lasso tool

Select the Lasso Tool and set Feather and selection mode options in the options bar.

Drag to draw a freehand selection border around your subject.

Hold Alt (Windows) or Option (macOS) and select points to draw straight-edged segments, then release to return to freehand drawing.

Release the mouse button to close the selection border automatically.

Tip

Press Delete while holding Alt or Option to erase recently drawn straight segments before closing the selection.

## Select with the Polygonal Lasso tool

Select the Polygonal Lasso tool and configure selection options in the options bar.

Select in the image to set the starting point.

Select again to set each endpoint of straight segments—continue selecting to build your selection border.

Hold Shift while selecting to constrain segments to multiples of 45 degrees.

Hold Alt (Windows) or Option (macOS) and drag to temporarily switch to freehand drawing.

Select the starting point when a closed circle appears next to the pointer or double-click to close the border.

## Select with the Magnetic Lasso tool

Select the Magnetic Lasso tool and set selection options in the options bar.

Set Width to define how far from the pointer the tool detects edges (press or to adjust width by 1 pixel).

Set Contrast (1–100%) to control edge-detection sensitivity—higher values detect sharper edges.

Set Frequency (0–100) to control how often fastening points anchor the border—higher values add more points.

Enable Stylus Pressure if using a tablet to decrease edge width with increased pressure.

Select in the image to place the first fastening point, then move the pointer along the edge you want to trace.

Select to manually add fastening points if the border doesn't snap to the desired edge.

Hold Alt (Windows) or Option (macOS) and drag to temporarily switch to freehand lasso drawing.

Double-click or select over the starting point to close the border or hold Alt/Option and double-click to close with a straight segment.

Note

The Magnetic Lasso tool is not available for 32-bits-per-channel images. It works best for objects with complex edges against high-contrast backgrounds.

Refine your selection

After creating any lasso selection, select Select>Select and Mask to refine the selection boundary with advanced edge-detection controls.


====================================================================================================

## 195. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/make-selections/get-started-selections/select-non-transparent-or-unmasked-areas.html

Use modifier keys with layer thumbnails to load selections based on pixel opacity or mask boundaries.

Select visible pixels from a layer or mask by using modifier keys with layer thumbnails. This method lets you quickly create selections based on pixel opacity or mask boundaries.

Use this technique to isolate layer content surrounded by transparency or work with existing mask boundaries. The resulting selection automatically matches the opacity of the source - fully opaque areas produce hard edges while partially transparent areas create feathered selections. For selection refinement techniques, see the [Select and Mask workspace](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/select-mask.html). To understand mask fundamentals, see [Add layer masks](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/masking-layers.html).

Layer thumbnails in the Layers panel show actual image content, while mask thumbnails display grayscale values where white reveals and black conceals. These thumbnails serve as visual references for what each selection operation captures.

## Load a new selection from layer content or masks

In the Layers panel, locate the layer with the content or mask you want to select.

Select all non-transparent pixels on a layer: Press Ctrl (Windows) or Command (macOS) and click the layer thumbnail.

Select unmasked areas from a layer mask: Press Ctrl (Windows) or Command (macOS) and click the layer mask thumbnail.

Areas with partial transparency create feathered selection edges, while fully opaque areas produce hard-edged selections.

## Modify existing selections with layer content or masks

Create or load a selection using any selection tool.

To add pixels to the selection, press Ctrl + Shift (Windows) or Command + Shift (macOS) and click the layer or mask thumbnail.

To subtract pixels from the selection, press Ctrl + Alt (Windows) or Command + Option (macOS) and click the layer or mask thumbnail.

To select the layer or mask, press Ctrl + Alt + Shift (Windows) or Command + Option + Shift (macOS), then click the layer or mask thumbnail.

To move or transform all layer contents including transparent areas, use the Move tool directly without loading a selection. Loading transparency as a selection is necessary only when you want to isolate the visible pixels for editing, copying, or masking.

When working with layer groups, clicking the group folder thumbnail selects all non-transparent pixels across all visible layers within that group, making it efficient to create composite selections from multiple layers simultaneously.


====================================================================================================

## 196. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/make-selections/get-started-selections/make-selections-with-marquee-tools.html

Draw rectangular, elliptical, or single-pixel selections with geometric precision.

Marquee tools create geometric selections that isolate specific areas of your image for editing, applying effects, or copying content. Use these tools when you need regularly shaped selections, such as rectangles, circles, or single-pixel rows and columns. For irregular shapes or complex edges, consider using the Lasso tools or the Object Selection tool instead.

The Marquee toolset includes four tools:  for rectangular or square selections,  for elliptical or circular selections, and Single Row or Single Column Marquee for 1-pixel-wide selections. Marquee selections can be combined, subtracted, or intersected to create more complex selection shapes.

## Select your marquee tool

The  tool creates rectangular selections or perfect squares when the Shift key is pressed. The  tool creates elliptical selections or perfect circles with Shift. The Single Row Marquee and Single Column Marquee tools define 1-pixel-wide selections as rows or columns.

## Configure tool options

You can enable snapping to help align selections with document elements by choosing View>Snap, or choosing View>Snap To and selecting guides, grids, slices, or document bounds from the submenu. If the Single Row or Single Column Marquee is not visible, increase the magnification of your image view.


====================================================================================================

## 197. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/make-selections/automatic-color-based-selections/improved-select-subject-and-remove-background-results.html

Learn how to make detailed selections and edit images quickly with improved device and cloud processing in professional photoeditor.

![Image 1: Girl wearing headphones with her hair flying upward, showing improved hair selection using Select Subject with device processing in photoeditor.](https://helpx-prod.scene7.com/is/image/HelpxProdLoc/nfs-MAX-ps-d-select-subject-sept2025?$pjpeg$&jpegSize=300&wid=1920)

Make detailed selections with improved device processing in Select Subject and Remove Background. 

## Improve results with on-device processing

Select File>Open to browse and open an image.

Select Settings**>**Image Processing (macOS) or Edit**>**Preferences**>**Image Processing (Windows) from the main menu.

Select Device from the Select Subject and Remove Background dropdown in the Preferences dialog box.

By default, photoeditor uses Device processing to refine selections.

![Image 2: photoeditor Preferences dialog showing Image Processing settings with Device (quicker results) selected for improved Select Subject and Remove Background results](https://helpx-prod.scene7.com/is/image/HelpxProdLoc/ps-improved-select-subject-and-remove-background-revised?$pjpeg$&jpegSize=200&wid=1200)

Select Device as the processing mode in Preferences > Image Processing for improved results.

In the Select and Mask workspace, refine the selection and apply edits.

## Improve results with cloud processing

Select File>Open to browse and open an image.

Select Settings>Image Processing (macOS) or Edit>Preferences>Image Processing (Windows) from the main menu.

Select Cloud from the Select Subject and Remove Background dropdown in the Preferences dialog box.

In the Select and Mask workspace, refine the selection and apply edits.


====================================================================================================

## 198. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/make-selections/automatic-color-based-selections/make-precise-selections-using-select-people.html

Learn how to use the Select People tool in photoeditor to make accurate selections of individuals and their features.

Select the Object Selection tool from the toolbar.

Select Select people from the Options bar.

Select the thumbnail of one of the people in the image.

Select the Entire person to select their entire body or select the given tags for selections such as Facial skin, Beard, Hair, Eyebrows, and more.

Note

You can also select details directly on the canvas when hovering over it.

Learn how to remove unwanted objects from your image using the Delete and Fill Selection tool.

Select the object you want to remove using any selection tool that works best for your image.

Control+click (macOS) or right-click (Windows)the selected object. Then, select Delete and Fill Selection.

photoeditor will automatically remove the selected object and fill the area with content that matches the surrounding image.

## 200. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/make-selections/automatic-color-based-selections/make-improved-hair-selections.html

Learn how to make cleaner, more accurate hair selections using the hair refinement feature in the Select and Mask workspace.

Select the Object Selection tool, then select Select Subject**.**

Select Select and Mask>Refine Hair.

Adjust the following settings in the Properties panel:

*   Transparency**:** Fine-tune the visibility of your selection over the image.
*   Preset**:** Apply commonly used refine settings.
*   Refine Mode**:** Switch between Color Aware and Object Aware for better edge detection.
*   Edge Detection**:** Set parameters to help photoeditor better identify fine hair edges.
*   Global Refinement**:** Adjust values for Smooth, Feather, Contrast, and Shift Edge for precise control.
*   Output Settings**:** Define how and where the final selection is applied or exported.

Select OK to apply the refined selection.

## 201. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/make-selections/automatic-color-based-selections/mask-all-objects-in-a-layer.html

Use the Mask All Objects feature to automatically create separate masks for each detected object within a layer.

Select a layer from the Layers panel.

Right-click (Windows) or Control-click (macOS) the selected layer.

Select Mask All Objects.
