## 318. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-corrections/adjust-color-and-tone-with-curves.html

Understand how Curves provides precise control over tonal range and color balance through graph-based adjustments.

When you need precise control over the brightness, contrast, and color balance in your images, Curves provides the most flexible adjustment tool in photoeditor. Unlike simpler tonal adjustments that offer limited control points, Curves lets you target up to 14 specific points across the entire tonal range, from the deepest shadows to the brightest highlights.

The power of Curves lies in its visual representation of how input values (your original image data) map to output values (your adjusted results). This graph-based approach reveals the relationship between tones and lets you reshape that relationship with precision that other adjustment methods cannot match.

## How the Curves graph works

The Curves adjustment displays your image's tonal information as a diagonal line on a graph. For RGB images, the horizontal axis represents input levels (original brightness values from 0 to 255), while the vertical axis represents output levels (adjusted values). The lower-left corner represents pure black (0), and the upper-right corner represents pure white (255).

Initially, this line appears as a straight 45-degree diagonal, indicating that input values match output values exactly, no adjustment has been applied. When you add control points to this line and move them, you create a curve that redistributes tonal values across your image.

The shape of the curve directly corresponds to the tonal changes in your image. Steeper sections of the curve represent areas of increased contrast, while flatter sections represent reduced contrast. Moving a point upward lightens the corresponding tonal range, while moving it downward darkens that range.

Curves can also adjust CMYK, LAB, and Grayscale images. For CMYK images, the graph displays ink percentages rather than brightness values.

## Tonal zones and control points

The curve divides into three primary zones that correspond to different brightness ranges in your image:

*   Shadows occupy the lower-left portion of the curve, representing the darkest tones
*   Midtones fill the center section, containing the bulk of tonal information in most images
*   Highlights appear in the upper-right area, representing the brightest tones

When you place a control point on the curve and drag it, you primarily affect the tonal zone where that point sits. However, curves are continuous functions adjusting one point influences neighboring tones, creating smooth transitions rather than abrupt jumps. This interconnected behavior is what makes Curves both powerful and nuanced.

You can add up to 14 control points along the curve, allowing targeted adjustments to specific brightness ranges without affecting the entire image. Removing a control point is as simple as dragging it off the graph.

## Channel-specific adjustments

Beyond overall tonal corrections, Curves excels at precise color balance adjustments. By switching from the composite RGB channel to individual Red, Green, or Blue channels, you can target color casts in specific tonal ranges. For example, you might add warmth only to the highlights while keeping shadows neutral, or remove a cyan cast from midtones without affecting other areas.

This channel-level control makes Curves particularly valuable for color grading workflows and correcting complex color issues that affect only certain brightness ranges.

## Curve shapes and their effects

Different curve shapes produce distinct visual results:

S-curves increase contrast by steepening the midtone portion of the curve while slightly compressing shadows and highlights. This creates more separation between similar tones, making images appear punchier and more dynamic.

Inverted curves produce negative or solarized effects by mapping bright input values to dark outputs and vice versa.

Flat curves reduce contrast by decreasing the slope, creating softer, lower-contrast images that can work well for certain aesthetic approaches or as preparation for further adjustments.

## Interactive adjustment tools

The On-image adjustment tool lets you click directly on your image to place control points at the exact tonal values you want to modify. Instead of guessing where a particular tone falls on the curve, you can target it directly and then drag up or down in the image to adjust that range. This direct manipulation approach makes Curves more intuitive, especially when working with complex images.

## Black and white point mapping

The endpoints of the curve define the darkest and brightest values in your adjusted image. Moving the lower-left point (black point) to the right remaps your darkest shadows to a lighter value, reducing overall contrast. Moving the upper-right point (white point) to the left remaps your brightest highlights to a darker value, also reducing contrast.

The black point and white point sliders beneath the curve provide another way to set these endpoints by defining which input values should be mapped to pure black (0) or pure white (255). This approach is particularly useful for setting proper tonal range when scanning images or working with files that don't use the full available range of values.

## Display options and visual feedback

Curves offers several display options that help you understand how adjustments affect your image:

The histogram overlay shows the distribution of tonal values in your original image, revealing whether your photo contains more shadows, midtones, or highlights. This information guides where to place control points for maximum effect.

The baseline reference displays the original 45-degree line underneath your adjusted curve, making it easy to see how far you've moved from the neutral starting point.

Channel overlays display individual color channel curves on top of the composite curve when working in RGB mode, providing a complete picture of how each channel contributes to the overall adjustment.

Toggle between simple and detailed grid displays to either focus on the curve shape or make precise measurements at 10% intervals.

## Presets and auto corrections

Curves includes preset adjustments that apply common correction patterns with a single click. These presets range from basic contrast enhancements to dramatic color grades, providing starting points you can further refine.

The Auto button applies automatic tonal correction based on the image content, often producing good results as a starting point. You can customize how Auto corrections work by accessing the Auto Color Correction Options, where you can specify whether to prioritize contrast, color balance, or both.

## Adjustment layers versus direct adjustments

Applying Curves as an adjustment layer preserves your original image data and allows future modifications. The adjustment remains editable, and you can toggle its visibility, adjust opacity, or delete it entirely without affecting the underlying pixels.

Applying Curves directly to an image layer (via Image>Adjustments>Curves) permanently modifies pixel data, discarding the original information. This destructive approach limits your flexibility for future edits but can be appropriate when working with temporary compositions or when you need to finalize edits before delivery.

## Relationship to other tonal adjustments

While Levels provides similar tonal control through input and output sliders, Curves offers greater precision with multiple control points along the tonal range. Levels works well for straightforward corrections like setting black and white points or adjusting overall brightness, while Curves excels when you need to target specific tonal zones independently.

For color-specific adjustments beyond tonal corrections, [Hue/Saturation adjustments](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/adjusting-hue-saturation.html) provide targeted control over individual color ranges. When you need to save complex Curves adjustments for reuse across multiple images, learn about [saving adjustment settings as presets](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/color-adjustments.html#save_adjustment_settings).

## Keyboard shortcuts for efficient workflow

Curves supports keyboard shortcuts that speed up common operations. You can select multiple control points with Shift-click, navigate between points using the plus and minus keys, and move selected points with arrow keys. When working with multiple color channels, Shift + Ctrl-click (Windows) or Shift + Command-click (Mac) in the image places control points in all component channels simultaneously.

These shortcuts become particularly valuable when making subtle, iterative adjustments where precise control point positioning matters.


====================================================================================================

## 319. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-corrections/adjust-color-saturation-using-vibrance-adjustment.html

Use a Vibrance adjustment layer to enhance muted colors while preserving skin tones and preventing color clipping.

Vibrance provides intelligent saturation control that adapts to the existing color intensity in your image, making it ideal for enhancing photos with people or scenes containing both muted and already-saturated colors.

Context  
 Use a Vibrance adjustment layer when you want to boost color intensity without oversaturating already-vivid colors or affecting skin tones. This non-destructive approach preserves your original image data and allows ongoing adjustments. For uniform saturation changes across all colors regardless of their current intensity, consider using a [Hue/Saturation adjustments](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-corrections/apply-a-hue-or-saturation-adjustment.html) layer instead.

## Create and adjust a Vibrance layer

Ensure you have an image open in photoeditor 2023 or later with colors you want to enhance.

Select Layer>New Adjustment Layer>Vibrance, or select the Vibrance icon in the Adjustments panel.

In the Properties panel, drag the Vibrance slider to the right to enhance muted colors while protecting already-saturated colors, or drag left to reduce intensity.

Adjust the Saturation slider if you need to apply uniform saturation changes across all colors regardless of their current intensity.

While you can apply Vibrance directly by selecting Image>Adjustments>Vibrance, this method permanently modifies your image pixels. Using an adjustment layer preserves your original image and lets you modify or remove the adjustment at any time.

The Vibrance adjustment layer appears in your Layers panel, where you can adjust its opacity, toggle visibility, or delete it without affecting your original image. Double-click the adjustment layer thumbnail to reopen the Properties panel and modify your settings.

## 320. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-corrections/adjust-color-tone-levels-curves-eyedroppers.html

Learn how to use eyedropper tools to define tonal range by sampling image areas and mapping them to target values.

Eyedropper tools let you define black points, white points, or neutral midtones by sampling directly from your image.

This approach works best when you can identify clear reference points—areas that should be pure black, pure white, or neutral gray. For broader tonal adjustments using sliders or curve points, see the [Levels adjustment](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/levels-adjustment.html) and [Curves adjustment](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/curves-adjustment.html).

The Set Gray Point eyedropper is unavailable for grayscale images because it's designed for color correction.

*   Apply a Levels or Curves adjustment layer to your image
*   Identify target areas in your image (darkest shadow, brightest highlight, or neutral gray)

The Set Gray Point eyedropper is unavailable for grayscale images because it's designed specifically for color correction.

## Set black and white points

Apply a Curves or Levels adjustment layer from the Adjustments panel.

In the Properties panel, double-click the Set Black Point eyedropper to configure shadow targets, or double-click the Set White Point eyedropper to configure highlight targets.

In the professional Color Picker, set R, G, and B to 0 for pure black, or to 255 for pure white. For gray tones, enter identical values between 0 and 255—lower values create darker grays, higher values create lighter grays.

Select OK to save your target values.

Click the darkest area that should contain detail with the Set Black Point eyedropper, or click the brightest area with the Set White Point eyedropper.

Don't sample specular highlights (bright reflections on shiny surfaces like glass or metal) as white points—these areas should remain pure white with no detail.

## Remove color casts with the gray point eyedropper

Identify an area in your image that should be neutral gray, such as pavement, clouds, or white fabric in shadow.

Apply a Levels or Curves adjustment layer from the Adjustments panel.

In the Properties panel, double-click the Set Gray Point eyedropper.

In the professional Color Picker, verify that R, G, and B values are identical—for example, 128, 128, 128. If needed, enter identical values to ensure a neutral target.

Select OK to confirm.

With the Set Gray Point eyedropper active, click the neutral area you identified in your image.

Use a color sampler to mark neutral areas before applying the adjustment. This makes them easier to select with the eyedropper.

The adjustment resets midtones and removes the color cast throughout the image. After applying eyedropper corrections, refine your results with additional adjustments. Use the Levels sliders to modify contrast, or add control points to the Curves adjustment to fine-tune specific tonal ranges.


====================================================================================================

## 321. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-corrections/adjust-levels-in-an-image.html

Learn how to use histogram sliders to correct shadows, midtones, and highlights while neutralizing color casts.

The Levels adjustment gives you precise control over image tonal range and color balance by mapping intensity levels of shadows, midtones, and highlights to new values.

Use Levels when your image lacks contrast, appears too dark or light, or has an unwanted color cast. This adjustment redistributes pixel values across the full tonal range, increasing overall contrast and improving color accuracy. For images requiring localized tonal corrections or more complex curve adjustments, use the Curves adjustment instead.

The Levels histogram displays the distribution of pixels at each brightness level. The interface includes Input Level sliders (black, gray, and white) below the histogram and Output Level sliders below those. The outer two Input sliders map the black point and white point to the Output slider settings, which default to level 0 (black) and level 255 (white). Moving the black Input slider to the right maps darker pixels to level 0, while moving the white Input slider to the left maps lighter pixels to level 255. The middle Input slider adjusts gamma by moving the midtone without dramatically altering highlights and shadows.

To read the histogram display, see [About histograms](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/viewing-histograms-pixel-values.html).

*   professional photoeditor 2023 or later installed
*   An image opens in RGB, CMYK, or Grayscale color mode
*   Basic understanding of image histograms recommended

Try it in the app

Practice these Level adjustment techniques in photoeditor to see immediate results in your images.

Open photoeditor

## Adjust shadows, midtones, and highlights

When you move the black point slider to level 5, photoeditor maps all pixels at level 5 and lower to level 0. Moving the white point slider to level 243 maps all pixels at or above level 243 to level 255.

This mapping affects the darkest and lightest pixels in each channel and adjusts corresponding pixels in other channels proportionately to maintain color balance. Clipped shadows appear as black pixels with no detail; clipped highlights appear as white pixels with no detail.

You can also enter values directly in the first and third Input Levels text boxes or enter a gamma value in the middle Input Levels box for precise adjustments.

## Neutralize color casts

Select Auto in the Properties panel to apply the default adjustment. To experiment with different algorithms, choose Auto Options from the Properties panel menu, then change the algorithm in the Auto Color Correction Options dialog box. To achieve a neutral gray, set all color components to equal values. For example, assign equal red, green, and blue values in an RGB image.

To adjust tones for a specific color channel, choose an option from the Channel menu in the Properties panel. You can also Shif + click multiple channels in the Channels panel before choosing Image>Adjustments>Levels to edit channel combinations simultaneously. This method only works with direct adjustments, not adjustment layers.

## Increase overall contrast

If the histogram shows gaps at either end, drag the Input sliders to the edges of the actual pixel data. This redistributes tonal values from pure black to pure white, adding contrast.

While adjustment layers provide non-destructive editing, you can apply Levels directly to an image layer. Choose Layer>New Adjustment Layer>Levels and select OK in the New Layer dialog box to create an adjustment layer. Alternatively, choose Image>Adjustments>Levels to make direct adjustments. Choosing Image>Adjustments>Levels permanently modifies pixel information. Use adjustment layers whenever possible to maintain editing flexibility.

After making Levels adjustments, view the updated histogram in the Histogram panel to verify that your changes have improved the tonal distribution without excessive clipping.

For information about saving Level settings as presets for reuse, see Save adjustment settings and Reapply adjustment settings.


====================================================================================================

## 322. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-corrections/apply-brightness-and-contrast-adjustment.html

Use simple sliders to adjust tonal range and image contrast non-destructively.

The Brightness/Contrast adjustment provides a straightforward way to lighten or darken your image and expand or compress its tonal range. Use this adjustment when you need quick brightness and contrast changes without precise control over specific tonal ranges.

For more precise control over shadows, midtones, and highlights separately, consider using Levels or Curves adjustments instead. Brightness/Contrast works best for simple corrections and works non-destructively when applied as an adjustment layer.

## Create a Brightness/Contrast adjustment layer

## Understand normal and Legacy modes

When Use Legacy is selected in the Properties panel, the adjustment uniformly shifts all pixel values. This approach can cause loss of detail in bright or dark areas and is not recommended for photographic images. However, Legacy mode may be useful for editing masks or scientific imagery that require uniform shifts.

Use Legacy is automatically selected when you open Brightness/Contrast adjustment layers created in earlier versions of photoeditor.

## Apply adjustments directly to a layer

You can apply Brightness/Contrast directly to an image layer by selecting Image > Adjustments > Brightness/Contrast. This method permanently modifies the pixels on that layer and discards image information, making it difficult to refine your edits later.

For greater flexibility, use adjustment layers instead. Adjustment layers let you modify settings at any time without affecting your original image data.


====================================================================================================

## 323. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-corrections/apply-color-balance-and-photo-filter-adjustments.html

Learn how to use tonal sliders to correct color casts across shadows, midtones, and highlights non-destructively.

Correcting color imperfections in specific tonal ranges helps your photos achieve professional polish and visual impact.

Use Color Balance to shift colors across specific tonal ranges (shadows, midtones, highlights) with precision sliders. This adjustment works best as an adjustment layer, preserving your original image and letting you refine settings at any time. For comprehensive color corrections involving multiple techniques, see [work with adjustment and fill layers](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/create-manage-layers/color-adjustment-fill-layers/work-with-adjustment-and-fill-layers.html).

## Apply Color Balance as an adjustment layer

In the Adjustments panel, select the Color Balance icon, or select Layer>New Adjustment Layer>Color Balance.

In the Properties panel, select a tonal range: Shadows, Midtones, or Highlights.

Drag the Cyan/Red, Magenta/Green, or Yellow/Blue slider toward the color you want to add or away from the color you want to reduce. Values range from ‑100 to +100.

Optional: Clear the Preserve Luminosity checkbox to have color shifts affect brightness values. By default, this option maintains overall tonal balance.

Target midtones first for general color correction, then refine shadows and highlights for greater control.

To apply Color Balance destructively (permanently altering pixel data), select Image>Adjustments>Color Balance. This method discards original image information and limits future editing flexibility.

The Color Balance adjustment layer appears above your image layer in the Layers panel. Double-click the adjustment layer thumbnail to reopen the Properties panel and modify settings at any time. Use the layer mask to apply the adjustment to specific image areas or adjust layer opacity to reduce the overall effect strength.


====================================================================================================

## 324. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-corrections/color-adjustment-basics.html

Learn the essential preparation steps and workflows for making effective color corrections in your images in professional photoeditor.

Color and tonal adjustments are essential to professional image editing. Whether you are correcting color casts, recovering shadow detail, or preparing images for print, understanding these principles helps you make precise edits while preserving image quality.

Strong results also depend on an efficient workflow. Choices such as using adjustment layers instead of editing pixels directly, or reviewing the histogram before making corrections, help preserve flexibility and avoid irreversible changes.

## Essential preparation before adjusting

Your editing setup and file preparation directly affect the accuracy and flexibility of color adjustments. Proper preparation helps prevent common issues and preserves image quality throughout the editing process.

*   Monitor calibration ensures that colors display consistently across screens and in print. Without calibration, you may overcorrect images that already appear accurate on properly calibrated devices. For color-critical work, regular calibration is essential.
*   Adjustment layers provide a non-destructive workflow by keeping corrections separate from the original pixels. This approach lets you revise, hide, or stack adjustments at any time without degrading image quality. Although adjustment layers increase file size and RAM usage, they offer greater flexibility for professional editing.
*   Bit depth determines how much editing latitude an image retains. A 16-bit image stores far more color information than an 8-bit image, reducing the risk of banding and preserving smoother tonal transitions during aggressive adjustments. For best results, capture and edit images in 16-bit mode until the final output.
*   Creating a duplicate of the original file gives you a reliable backup if you need to restart corrections or repurpose the source image.
*   The Histogram and Info panels provide objective feedback while editing. The Histogram panel helps identify clipping, tonal range issues, and color casts, while the Info panel displays precise color and tonal values.
*   Masks allow selective adjustments so you can apply corrections to specific areas without affecting the entire image. This targeted control becomes increasingly important in complex editing workflows.

## General correction workflow

A structured approach to color correction produces more consistent results than fixing issues individually. Follow this workflow to build corrections on a solid foundation:

1.   **Evaluate the histogram** to understand the image’s tonal distribution. Look for clipping, missing tonal ranges, or imbalances in shadows, midtones, and highlights. 
2.   **Correct color balance** to remove unwanted color casts and establish neutral whites, grays, and blacks before making tonal adjustments. 
3.   **Adjust tonal range** with Levels or Curves to set white and black points, then refine midtones for overall brightness and contrast. For targeted corrections, use the Shadow/Highlight command to adjust shadow or highlight detail independently. 
4.   **Apply selective refinements** to enhance specific colors or create creative effects after establishing balanced color and tone. 
5.   **Sharpen the image** near the end of the workflow. Sharpening enhances existing detail but can also amplify noise and artifacts if applied too early. 
6.   **Optimize for output** when preparing images for print by adjusting highlight and shadow values to match the output device’s reproducible range and preserve detail.

## Working with RGB and CMYK color modes

The color mode you choose affects both image quality and editing flexibility. RGB mode provides a wider color gamut than CMYK, and photoeditor adjustment tools work most effectively with RGB data. For best results, perform most color and tonal corrections in RGB mode, even when the final output is intended for CMYK printing.

Avoid repeated conversions between RGB and CMYK, as each conversion discards some image data through color rounding. For print workflows, complete corrections in RGB first, then convert to CMYK and apply only necessary final adjustments.

Soft-proofing lets you preview how RGB colors will reproduce in CMYK without permanently converting the image. Set a proof profile that matches your target output device, then use Window>Arrange>New Window to create a second view of your image, and compare RGB and CMYK previews side by side in separate document windows. This approach helps you refine RGB adjustments while monitoring print output.

Colors visible in RGB but not reproducible in CMYK are considered out-of-gamut. photoeditor can identify these colors and help you adjust them before printing.


====================================================================================================

## 325. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-corrections/apply-auto-tone-auto-contrast-and-auto-color.html

Use adjustment layers with Auto options to quickly correct contrast, tone, and color casts in your images.

professional photoeditor offers three automatic correction commands that analyze your image and apply tonal adjustments: Auto Tone, Auto Contrast, and Auto Color. Each uses different algorithms to improve image quality.

These automatic corrections work best with photographic or continuous-tone images that need quick contrast or color balance improvements. For precise control over specific tonal ranges, use manual Levels or Curves adjustments instead. The non-destructive adjustment layer method described here preserves your original image data and lets you customize Auto options before applying them.

## Apply Auto Contrast for neutral adjustments

Auto Contrast clips shadow and highlight values without adjusting individual color channels, preserving the overall color balance while making highlights lighter and shadows darker.

In the Adjustments panel, select the Levels or Curves icon.

In the Properties panel, Alt-click (Windows) or Option-click (macOS) the Auto button.

Under Algorithms in the Auto Color Correction Options dialog, select Enhance Monochromatic Contrast.

Enter clipping percentages in the Shadows and Highlights text boxes. Values between 0.0% and 1% work best for most images.

Select the midtones color swatch to adjust the target neutral gray if needed.

Select OK to apply the correction.

By default, photoeditor clips 0.1% of shadow and highlight pixels. Lower values preserve more detail in extreme tones, while higher values create more dramatic contrast.

## Apply Auto Tone for maximum contrast

Auto Tone adjusts each color channel independently to maximize tonal range. This produces dramatic corrections but may introduce or remove color casts.

In the Adjustments panel, select the Levels or Curves icon.

In the Properties panel, Alt-click (Windows) or Option-click (macOS) the Auto button.

Under Algorithms, select Enhance Per Channel Contrast.

Adjust the clipping percentages and midtone target color as needed.

Select OK to apply the correction.

## Remove color casts with Auto Color

Auto Color identifies shadows, midtones, and highlights in your image, then neutralizes midtones and adjusts the tonal range. This correction removes color casts while improving overall contrast.

In the Adjustments panel, select the Levels or Curves icon.

In the Properties panel, Alt-click (Windows) or Option-click (macOS) the Auto button.

Under Algorithms, select Find Dark & Light Colors.

Select the Snap Neutral Midtones option.

Enter clipping percentages for shadows and highlights.

Select the shadows, midtones, or highlights color swatch to set custom target colors if the defaults don't produce the desired result.

Select OK to apply Auto Color.

Auto Color uses RGB 128 gray as the default midtone target. Adjusting this target lets you compensate for specific lighting conditions or creative color preferences.

## Save custom Auto correction settings

Save your preferred Auto Color Correction settings as defaults to use them consistently across multiple images.

In the Auto Color Correction Options dialog, configure your preferred algorithm, clipping values, and target colors.

Select Save as Defaults.

Select OK.

After saving defaults, the Auto button in the Properties panel and the Image>Auto Tone, Auto Contrast, and Auto Color commands use your custom settings.

## Apply corrections directly to image layers

For quick corrections when you don't need adjustment layer flexibility, use the direct menu commands. Keep in mind this method permanently modifies pixel data and discards image information.

Select the image layer you want to correct.

Choose Image>Auto Tone, Auto Contrast, or Auto Color.

Direct image adjustments are destructive and cannot be modified after applying subsequent edits. Use adjustment layers for non-destructive workflows.

For more control over tonal adjustments, learn about [Levels adjustments](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/levels-adjustment.html) and [Curves adjustments](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/curves-adjustment.html).


====================================================================================================

## 326. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-corrections/use-the-histogram-panel.html

Learn how to monitor tonal distribution and customize display options to evaluate image quality and track adjustments in real time in professional photoeditor.

The Histogram panel shows how pixels are distributed across tonal values, helping you evaluate image quality and make accurate corrections.

Use the Histogram panel to assess tonal range, identify clipped shadows or highlights, and monitor adjustments in real time. The panel provides multiple view modes, channel options, and statistical data. To inspect specific pixel color values, use the [Info panel with the Eyedropper and Color Sampler tools](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/viewing-histograms-pixel-values.html).

## Understanding histogram displays

A histogram graphs the number of pixels at each intensity level. The left side represents shadows, the center shows midtones, and the right side displays highlights. The histogram shape reveals the tonal distribution of your image:

*   Low-key images contain more shadow detail and appear weighted to the left.
*   High-key images contain more highlight detail and appear weighted to the right.
*   Average-key images concentrate detail in the midtone range.
*   Images with a full tonal range show pixel distribution across the entire histogram.

Pixels grouped at either edge indicate clipping, which causes loss of detail in shadows or highlights. Images with a full tonal range generally provide greater editing flexibility.

For RGB and CMYK images, the histogram displays overlapping color channels. Areas where all channels overlap appear white, while two-channel overlaps appear as cyan, magenta, or yellow.

## Open and configure the panel view

Select Window>Histogram to open the panel.

Select a view option from the panel menu.

**View options****Description**
Compact View Displays only the histogram graph.
Expanded View Adds channel selection, statistics, source options, and refresh controls.
All Channels View Displays individual color channel histograms along with all Expanded View features, excluding alpha, spot, and masks.

The panel retains your channel selection when switching between Expanded View and All Channels View.

## Select channels to display

In Expanded View or All Channels View, select the Channel drop-down menu, and then choose the channel option that matches your analysis needs.

**Channel options****Description**
RGB Displays a combined view of all channels.
Red or Green, or Blue Displays the view of the selected channel.
Luminosity Displays luminance values of the composite channel.
Colors Displays color channels overlaid in their respective colors.

In All Channels View, the Channel drop-down menu controls only the top histogram display.

## Display channels in color

In Expanded View or All Channels View, select the panel menu, and then select Show Channels in Color.

When enabled, individual channels appear in their respective colors. If you select a single channel from the Channel drop-down menu with this option active, that channel displays in color even when switching to Compact View. Similarly, choosing Colors from the Channel drop-down menu displays the composite histogram in color across all views.

Channels display in their respective colors. If you select a single channel from the Channel drop-down menu, that channel remains colorized even in Compact View. Selecting Colors from the Channel drop-down menu displays the composite histogram in color across all views.

## View histogram statistics

In Expanded View or All Channels View, select the panel menu, and then select Show Statistics if statistics are not visible.

Position your pointer over any part of the histogram to view values for that intensity level.

Drag across the histogram to highlight a range and view statistics for that tonal range.

**Statistic****Description**
Mean Average intensity value.
Std Dev Variation in intensity values.
Median Middle intensity value.
Pixels Total number of pixels used to generate the histogram.
Level Intensity level under the pointer.
Count Number of pixels at the selected intensity level.
Percentile Percentage of pixels at or below the selected level, from 0% at the far left to 100% at the far right.
Cache Level Image cache level used to generate the histogram.

## Select a source for multilayer documents

In Expanded View, select the Source drop-down menu, and then choose the histogram source as needed for evaluation.

**Source options****Description**
Entire Image Displays the histogram data for all layers combined.
Selected Layer Displays the histogram for only the currently selected layer in the Layers panel
Adjustment Composite Displays the histogram for a selected adjustment layer plus all layers below it.

The Source menu appears only in documents with multiple layers.

## Preview adjustment effects

In the Adjustment dialog box, open any color or tonal, and select the Preview option.

The Histogram panel updates in real time to show how adjustments affect tonal distribution. When using adjustment layers in the Adjustments panel, changes appear automatically without requiring a Preview option.

The panel overlays the original and adjusted histograms, making tonal shifts easy to compare.

## Refresh cached histogram data

When a histogram is generated from cached data instead of the current image state, the Cached Data Warning icon appears in the Histogram panel. Cached histograms render faster by sampling representative pixels based on the current zoom level. Cache levels range from 1, which uses all image pixels, to 8, which prioritizes faster rendering by sampling more pixels. Higher cache settings improve performance for large multilayer files but require more RAM.

Select the panel menu, and then select the Uncached Refresh option, or double-click anywhere in the histogram.

You can adjust the maximum cache level in Edit>Preferences>Performance (Windows) or photoeditor>Settings>Performance (macOS). Use lower cache levels if working primarily with smaller images or if system RAM is limited.


====================================================================================================

## 327. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/selective-color-adjustments/match-color-in-different-images.html

Understand how the Match Color command in professional photoeditor helps you make colors consistent between images, layers, or selections.

The Match Color command in photoeditor adjusts colors in single or multiple images, layers, or selections to align with colors in another. This feature helps create visual consistency across multiple photos or elements. It can be accessed from Image>Adjustments>Match Color.

### Overview of Match Color

Match Color is a photoeditor command that standardizes colors between different sources. It works only in RGB mode and can be applied in the following ways:

*   Between two separate images.
*   Between different layers in the same image.
*   Between multiple selections within an image.

### Benefits of Match Color

Match Color is useful when you need to:

*   Create a consistent look across several photos in a series.
*   Match specific tones, such as skin colors, between different images.
*   Combine layers or selections while keeping a uniform color balance.

### Functionality of Match Color

When activated, the command temporarily changes the pointer to the Eyedropper tool so you can sample colors. The Info panel displays pixel values in real time, giving you feedback on adjustments.

The command provides controls for:

*   Luminance adjustment to brighten or darken the image.
*   Color range adjustment to expand or reduce the spectrum of tones.
*   Cast neutralization to correct unwanted color tints.


====================================================================================================

## 328. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/selective-color-adjustments/match-color-between-two-images.html

Learn how to use the Match Color command in professional photoeditor to make the colors of one image consistent with another.

The Match Color command works only in RGB mode. It allows you to adjust luminance, color intensity, and color cast while matching colors between images. You can apply it to an entire image, a specific layer, or a selection.

Select the image you want to change and make it an active document. If you want to apply the adjustment to a specific layer, make that layer active.

Select Image>Adjustments>Match Color.

From the Source menu in the Image Statistics area, select the source image whose colors you want to apply to the target.

*   Select None to use the target image itself for color calculations.
*   From the Layer menu, select a specific layer in the source image, or select Merged to use all layers.

Once selected, adjust how it's used:

*   Select Ignore Selection when Applying Adjustment to apply the adjustment to the entire target image.
*   Select Use Selection in Source to Calculate Colors to base the adjustment only on the source selection.
*   Select Use Selection in Target to Calculate Adjustment to base the adjustment only on the target selection.

Select Neutralize to automatically remove any color cast in the target image.

Adjust the Luminance slider or enter a value (1–200, default 100) to change brightness.

To change saturation, adjust the Color Intensity slider or enter a value (1–200, default 100). A value of 1 converts the image to grayscale.

Adjust the Fade slider to control the strength of the adjustment. Moving it right reduces the effect.

Select OK to apply the changes.


====================================================================================================

## 329. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/selective-color-adjustments/match-color-of-two-layers-in-the-same-image.html

Learn how to use the Match Color command in professional photoeditor to make one layer’s colors consistent with another layer in the same image.

Use the Match Color command in photoeditor to adjust the colors of a layer so they match the colors of another layer in the same image. This helps maintain consistency when blending multiple layers into a single composition.

Select an area in the layer you want to match if you want to adjust only a specific region (for example, skin tones). If you don't select, the adjustment uses the colors of the entire source layer.

Select the target layer (the one you want to adjust) and then select Image>Adjustments>Match Color.

In the Match Color dialog, confirm that the Source menu image is the same as your target image.

From the Layer menu, select the source layer you want to match. Select Merged to match colors from all layers combined.

Once an option is selected, adjust how it's used:

*   Select Ignore Selection when Applying Adjustment to apply the adjustment to the entire target layer.
*   Select Use Selection in Source to Calculate Colors to calculate the adjustment from the selected area of the source layer.
*   Select Use Selection in Target to Calculate Adjustment to calculate adjustment only from the selected area of the target layer.

Select Neutralize to remove any color cast in the target layer.

Adjust the following settings as needed:

*   Luminance**:**Move the slider or enter a value (1–200, default 100) to change brightness.
*   Color Intensity**:** Move the slider or enter a value (1–200, default 100) to change saturation. A value of 1 converts the layer to grayscale.
*   Fade**:**Move the slider to control the strength of the adjustment. Moving it right reduces the effect.

Select OK to apply the changes.


====================================================================================================

## 330. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/selective-color-adjustments/save-and-apply-settings-in-match-color.html

Learn more about saving and applying color adjustment settings using the Match Color in professional photoeditor to maintain consistency across your images.

Select Image>Adjustments>Match Color.

In the Image Statistics section of the Match Color dialog, select Save Statistics.

Enter a name for the settings file, select Tag from the drop-down list, and save it to your desired location.

## Apply saved settings

Select Image>Adjustments>Match Color.

In the Image Statistics area of the Match Color dialog, select Load Statistics.

Browse to the saved settings file, then load it to apply the adjustments.

## 331. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/selective-color-adjustments/replace-object-colors-by-applying-a-hue-or-saturation-adjustment.html

Learn more about replacing the color of selected objects in professional photoeditor using Hue/Saturation adjustment layers for flexible, non-destructive editing.

You can use a Hue/Saturation adjustment layer to replace the colors of specific objects while keeping your edits non-destructive.

Select the object with the Quick Selection**tool**.

In the Adjustments**panel >**Single adjustments, select the Hue/Saturation**<insert icon here> icon**. The selection becomes a mask on the adjustment layer.

In the Properties**panel**, move the Hue and Saturation sliders to replace the object’s color. If the original color tints the new one, select Colorize and readjust the settings.

Move the Lightness slider at 0 to maintain contrast. To preserve both contrast and saturation, set the adjustment layer to **Hue** blending mode.

Paint on the mask with black or white to refine the affected area.


====================================================================================================

## 332. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/selective-color-adjustments/customize-indexed-color-tables.html

Learn how to modify color tables to remap colors, create gradients, and assign transparency.

Customizing indexed color tables gives you precise control over the limited palette used in indexed-color images. This technique works particularly well for pseudocolor images—scientific or medical visualizations that display data variations using color rather than grayscale—by mapping specific color ranges to data values.

Use the Color Table dialog to modify colors in existing indexed-color images. The dialog displays your image's complete palette as a grid of color swatches, allowing you to edit individual colors, replace color ranges, or apply predefined schemes. Your image must be in Indexed Color mode to customize its color table. To convert RGB or Grayscale images to indexed color, see [Convert a grayscale or RGB image to indexed color](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-modes/convert-a-grayscale-or-rgb-image-to-indexed-color.html).

## Edit individual colors

Open your indexed-color image in professional photoeditor on desktop.

Select Image>Mode>Color Table.

Click any color swatch in the grid to open the Color Picker.

Choose your replacement color and select OK.

Repeat for additional colors, then select OK to apply all changes.

## Replace color ranges

Select Image>Mode>Color Table.

Click and drag across multiple swatches to select a color range.

In the Color Picker, choose the first color for your gradient and select OK.

When the Color Picker reopens, choose the last color in your gradient, then select OK.

photoeditor creates a smooth gradient between your two colors across the selected range. This technique works well for creating smooth color transitions in pseudocolor images or applying temperature-based color schemes to scientific data.

## Assign color transparency

Select Image>Mode>Color Table.

Select the Eyedropper tool inside the Color Table dialog.

Click the color swatch you want to make transparent or click that color directly in your image.

Select OK to apply the transparency.

You can assign transparency to only one color at a time. Selecting a different color for transparency removes transparency from the previously selected color.

## Apply predefined tables

photoeditor includes several preset color tables designed for different purposes, from scientific visualization to cross-platform color consistency.

## Save custom tables

Select Image>Mode>Color Table.

From the Table menu, choose one of the predefined options.

Preview the result in your image and select OK to apply.

## Available predefined tables

*   Custom: Retains your current palette for manual editing.
*   Black Body: Simulates colors emitted by a heated black body radiator, progressing from black through red and orange to yellow and white. Useful for thermal imaging.
*   Grayscale: Distributes 256 shades evenly from black to white.
*   Spectrum: Displays the visible light spectrum from violet through blue, green, and yellow to orange and red, as if light passed through a prism.
*   System (Mac OS): Applies the standard 256-color macOS system palette.
*   System (Windows): Applies the standard 256-color Windows system palette.

Applying a predefined table replaces your current color table entirely. Any custom color modifications are lost unless you save the table first.

## Save custom color tables for reuse

You can save customized color tables as. ACT (professional Color Table) files for reuse across projects.

## Save a custom color table

After customizing your color table, select Save in the Color Table dialog.

Choose a location and enter a descriptive filename.

Select Save to create an .ACT file.

## Load a saved color table

Open an indexed-color image.

Select Image>Mode>Color Table.

Select Load in the Color Table dialog.

Navigate to your saved .ACT file and select Open.

When you load a different color table, professional photoeditor remaps each pixel to match the corresponding position in the new palette. This can produce dramatic visual changes depending on how different the two palettes are.

You can also load saved color tables into the Swatches panel by selecting Load Swatches from the Swatches panel menu. This makes custom color sets available across all color modes, not just indexed-color images.

For more control over color adjustments in other color modes, use the adjustment commands under Image >Adjustments.


====================================================================================================

## 334. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/selective-color-adjustments/mix-color-channels-with-channel-mixer.html

Learn how to redistribute grayscale values between color channels to create custom color effects and corrections.

Channel Mixer  adjustments give you precise control over color relationships by blending grayscale data from source channels into targeted output channels.

Use Channel Mixer  for precise color corrections, adjusting individual color channels without affecting overall image brightness. Each color channel (RGB or CMYK) contains grayscale data representing tonal values for that color component. The Channel Mixer  redistributes this grayscale data between channels, giving you granular control over color relationships.

For converting color images to grayscale or creating monochrome effects, use the [Black & White adjustment](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/convert-color-image-black-white.html) instead.

The Channel Mixer creates a non-destructive adjustment layer that preserves your original image. You can save your settings as presets and access default presets from the Properties panel.

## Access and configure Channel Mixer

When you select an output channel, photoeditor automatically sets that channel's source slider to 100% and all other source sliders to 0%. For example, selecting Red as the output channel sets Red to 100%, Green to 0%, and Blue to 0% in an RGB image.

## Adjust channel mixing values

The Total field displays the combined value of all source channel sliders. For optimal results, keep the total near 100%—values significantly above 100% may cause highlight clipping. photoeditor displays a warning icon when the total exceeds 100%.

Using negative values inverts the source channel before adding it to the output channel, creating color complement effects. This technique is useful for correcting color casts or creating creative color shifts.

## Use the Constant option

The Constant slider adjusts the grayscale value of the entire output channel, affecting overall brightness:

*   Negative values (-200% to 0%) add black, darkening the channel
*   Positive values (0% to +200%) add white, lightening the channel
*   A value of -200% makes the output channel completely black
*   A value of +200% makes the output channel completely white

Use Constant to fine-tune overall channel brightness after adjusting source channel ratios.

## Monitor the color adjustments

Watch the Total Value in the Properties panel as you adjust sliders. The combined percentage indicates how much grayscale data you're applying to the output channel. Values above 100% brighten the image but may lose highlight detail.

For each output channel you want to modify, choose it from the Output Channel menu and adjust its source sliders independently. Repeat this process to refine color relationships across multiple channels.

You can [save Channel Mixer settings as presets](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/color-adjustments.html#save_adjustment_settings) for reuse on other images. Learn more about saving adjustment settings and reapplying adjustment settings.

Color photography is indispensable when colors and shades in your image are vivid and distinctive, revealing fine details. Black and white photography, on the other hand, helps emphasize the subject and textures without distraction and adds drama to your images.

photoeditor applies a default [grayscale](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/color-modes/convert-an-image-to-another-color-mode.html)conversion to your image.

Save and share your results.

Select Dither to smooth the gradient and reduce banding, and Reverse to flip the gradient colors.

## 338. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/hdr-and-channel-manipulation/adjust-hdr-exposure-and-toning.html

Apply Exposure adjustments or HDR Toning to control highlights, shadows, and midtones in high-dynamic-range images.

These adjustments help you manage tonal range in HDR images by working in linear color space. While primarily designed for 32-bit HDR images, you can apply both techniques to 16-bit and 8-bit images to create HDR-like effects.

Exposure adjustments work non-destructively through adjustment layers, preserving your original image data and allowing you to modify or remove adjustments at any time. HDR Toning provides a comprehensive set of controls in a single dialog, but it works directly on your image layer and requires flattened layers. Choose Exposure adjustments for flexible, non-destructive editing, or use HDR Toning when you need access to advanced tone-mapping methods like Local Adaptation or Equalize Histogram.

For more information about working non-destructively with adjustment layers, see [Adjustment and fill layers](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/adjustment-fill-layers.html).

## Apply Exposure adjustment

Exposure works by performing calculations in linear color space (gamma 1.0) rather than your current color space. The adjustment includes eyedropper tools that adjust luminance values rather than affecting all color channels.

## Configure Exposure settings

Ensure you have an image file(32-bit, 16-bit, or 8-bit) in RGB or Grayscale color mode and a flattened image with all layers merged for HDR Toning in photoeditor 2023 or later.Explore HDR exposure and toning adjustments directly in photoeditor to see real-time results on your images.

In the Adjustments panel, select the Exposure icon or an Exposure preset, or select Layer>New Adjustment Layer>Exposure.

In the Adjustments panel, adjust the Exposure slider to modify the highlight end of the tonal scale with minimal effect on shadows.

Adjust the Offset slider to darken shadows and midtones without significantly affecting highlights.

Adjust the Gamma slider to modify midtone values using a power function.

To set specific luminance values, select an eyedropper tool: Set Black Point shifts the clicked pixel to zero, Set White Point shifts the clicked pixel to white (1.0 for HDR images), or Midtone makes the clicked value middle gray.

With 32-bit images, access the Exposure slider at the bottom of the image window for quick adjustments without opening the Adjustments panel.

Avoid selecting Image > Adjustments > Exposure, which makes direct adjustments to your image layer and permanently discards image information.

## Apply HDR Toning

HDR Toning provides multiple tone-mapping methods including Local Adaptation, Equalize Histogram, Exposure and Gamma, and Highlight Compression. Each method offers different controls for managing conversion from HDR to lower bit depths.

## Configure HDR Toning settings

If your image has multiple layers, select Layer>Flatten Image to combine all layers.

Confirm your image is in RGB or Grayscale color mode by checking Image>Mode.

Select Image>Adjustments>HDR Toning.

In the HDR Toning dialog, select a Method from the dropdown menu and adjust the available settings to control contrast, exposure, and tonal range.

Select OK to apply the adjustments.

HDR Toning permanently modifies your image layer. Consider duplicating your image or creating a snapshot in the History panel before applying this adjustment to preserve the original state.

For detailed information about HDR Toning methods and specific settings for Edge Glow, Tone and Detail, Color controls, and the Toning Curve, see [High dynamic range images](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/high-dynamic-range-images.html).

## 339. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/hdr-and-channel-manipulation/set-up-hdr-display-support-in-photoeditor.html

Learn how to configure OS display settings and enable the Technology Previews preference to view HDR content on compatible displays.

HDR display support enhances your editing experience with greater brightness and contrast and lets you view photos with enhanced highlights and shadow detail.

Setup steps vary by operating system and display type. On macOS, HDR display is enabled by default in photoeditor but requires specific OS display settings. On Windows, both the OS settings and photoeditor preference must be configured manually. You must have Windows 11 or later to use this feature.

Before configuring HDR display support, verify that your system meets the requirements.

*   Compatible display (macOS): MacBook Pro 14" or 16" with XDR display (November 2021 or later) or Apple Pro Display XDR
*   Compatible display (Windows): VESA Certified DisplayHDR level 1000 or higher
*   Operating system (Windows): Windows 11 or later
*   Graphics processor (GPU): Enabled in photoeditor preferences

## Configure HDR on macOS with Apple HDR display

Select System Settings>Display>Presets on your HDR display or built-in Apple laptop display.

Select the Apple XDR Display (P3-1600 nits) preset.

Select photoeditor>Settings>Technology Previews.

Select the Precise color management for HDR Display checkbox to enable the feature.

This preset optimizes display brightness and color gamut for HDR content

## Configure HDR on macOS with non-Apple HDR display

Select System Settings>Display on your Mac.

Toggle ON the High Dynamic Range option.

Select photoeditor>Settings>Technology Previews.

Select the Precise color management for HDR Display checkbox to enable the feature.

Non-Apple HDR displays require manual activation in macOS system settings. Once enabled, photoeditor can display HDR content with precise color management.

## Configure HDR on Windows 11 with HDR display

Confirm you are running Windows 11 by selecting Start>Settings>System>About.

Select System>Display>HDR>Display Capabilities to verify your display supports HDR.

Select System>Display>HDR, and then toggle ON the Use HDR option.

Select Edit>Preferences>Technology Previews.

Select the Precise color management for HDR Display checkbox to enable the feature.

photoeditor HDR display support is only available on Windows 11. Earlier Windows versions do not support this feature.

After completing these configuration steps, photoeditor displays HDR images with accurate color representation and extended dynamic range. Your photos will show enhanced detail in both highlights and shadows when viewed on your calibrated display.

For advanced color pipeline workflows using 32-bit mode, see [OpenColorIO and tools with 32-bit mode](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/using/opencolorio-transform.html). For more information about viewing HDR content, [see photoeditor system requirements](https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/get-started/technical-requirements-installation/professional-photoeditor-on-desktop-technical-requirements.html).


====================================================================================================

## 340. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/hdr-and-channel-manipulation/blend-channels-using-calculations.html

Learn how to create creative composites by blending channel pixels with Apply Image and Calculations in professional photoeditor on desktop.

Apply Image works with single and composite channels, while Calculations operates exclusively on single channels.

Open your source and destination images, then select the target layer and channel in the destination image.

Select Image>Apply Image.

In the dialog box, choose your source image, layer, and channel. Select Merged for Layer to use all layers in the source.

Select Preview to view results.

Choose a blending mode from the Blending menu.

Enter an Opacity value between 1 and 100 to control effect strength.

Select Preserve Transparency to restrict blending to opaque areas only.

To blend with a mask, select Mask, then choose the image, layer, and channel that contain your mask. Select Invert to reverse which areas are protected and which are affected by the blending.

When color modes differ between images, apply individual channels rather than composite channels.

## Blend channels with Calculations

Open your source image or images with matching pixel dimensions.

Select Image>Calculations.

Select Preview to view results as you adjust settings.

Choose the first source image, layer, and channel. Select Merged for Layer to use all layers.

Optionally select Invert to use the channel's negative values or Gray to work with grayscale values.

Choose the second source image, layer, and channel. Optionally select Invert to use the channel's negative.

Choose a blending mode from the Blending menu.

Enter an Opacity value to specify effect strength.

To blend with a mask, select Mask, choose the image and layer containing the mask, then select the channel or transparency boundary. Select Invert for masks to reverse which areas are protected during blending.

When color modes differ between images, you can apply single channels only, not composite channels.

For Result, specify whether to create a new document, add a new channel, or select the active image.

## 341. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/hdr-and-channel-manipulation/channels-in-photoeditor.html

Learn how channels store color data, selections, and print plates in your images in professional photoeditor on desktop.

Channels form the foundation of how photoeditor stores and manages image information. Every image you work with contains channels that are grayscale representations that store different types of data, from the colors you see to the selections you save to specialized printing information.

Understanding channels helps you make informed decisions about file formats, manage complex selections, and troubleshoot color issues. While you may rarely interact with channels directly, they influence everything from your file sizes to how your images print.

## Three types of channels

photoeditor uses three distinct channel types, each serving a specific purpose in your workflow.

### Color information channels

Color information channels store the actual image data you see on screen. photoeditor creates these automatically when you open or create a new image, with the number and type determined by your color mode.

An RGB image contains separate channels for red, green, and blue information, plus a composite channel that displays all colors combined. CMYK images have cyan, magenta, yellow, and black channels. Grayscale images use a single channel to represent tonal information.

Each color channel is a grayscale image where brightness values represent color intensity. In 8-bit images, these values range from 0 (no color) to 255 (maximum color intensity). The composite channel combines these individual channels to display the full-color image you edit.

### Alpha channels

Alpha channels store selections as grayscale images, preserving complex selection boundaries you've spent time refining. When you save a selection to an alpha channel, white areas represent selected regions, black areas represent masked regions, and gray values represent partial transparency.

These channels let you recall intricate selections across work sessions without recreating them. You can store multiple alpha channels in a single image, each preserving a different selection. Alpha channels also form the foundation of layer masks, though the two concepts serve different purposes in your workflow.

### Spot color channels

Spot color channels define additional printing plates for specialized inks beyond standard CMYK process colors. Print shops use these channels when your project requires precise color matching with premixed inks, such as Pantone colors, or when adding special finishes like metallic or varnish effects.

Each spot color channel specifies where that particular ink prints on the page. Unlike color information channels, spot channels don't contribute to the on-screen appearance unless you specifically view them. They exist solely for print production, translating to separate plates during output.

## Channels panel

The Channels panel displays all channels in your image, providing visibility into your image's underlying structure. Channels appear in a specific hierarchy that reflects how photoeditor processes your image data.

### Channel organization

For RGB, CMYK, and Lab images, the composite channel appears first, followed by individual color channels. Alpha channels appear after color channels, and spot color channels display last. This ordering matters for spot color printing, where channels overprint from top to bottom in the sequence shown.

Each channel displays a thumbnail preview showing its grayscale content. These thumbnails update automatically as you edit, helping you track changes. You can resize or hide thumbnails through the panel menu if you prefer a compact view or need improved performance.

### Channel visibility and selection

The eye icon next to each channel controls visibility in your document window. You can view channels individually to examine specific color information or view multiple channels simultaneously to see how they combine.

Selected channels appear highlighted in the panel. You can select multiple channels by shift-clicking, though you can paint or edit only one channel at a time. When all default color channels are visible, photoeditor automatically displays the composite channel.

## Channel limits and performance

photoeditor supports up to 56 channels per image, including all color, alpha, and spot channels combined. Most images use far fewer—an RGB image with a few saved selections might contain only 6-8 channels total.

### File size considerations

Each channel adds to file size based on your image dimensions and bit depth. An alpha channel in a 1000×1000 pixel, 8-bit image adds approximately 1 megabyte. Larger images or higher bit depths increase this proportionally—a 16-bit image doubles the space required per channel.

Complex alpha channels with detailed masks can substantially increase disk space requirements. Before saving, consider whether you need all stored alpha channels or if you can delete unused selections.

The photoeditor document status bar shows total file size, including all channels and layers, when you select Document Sizes from the pop-up menu. This value reflects uncompressed size; actual saved file size varies based on format and compression settings.

## File format compatibility

Your choice of file format determines which channel types photoeditor preserves when saving.

### Color channel preservation

All standard formats preserve color channels as long as they support your image's color mode. Saving an RGB image as JPEG preserves the three color channels, though JPEG's compression may reduce quality.

### Alpha channel preservation

Only specific formats maintain alpha channels: photoeditor (PSD), photoeditor Large Document Format (PSB), PDF, TIFF, and Raw formats. Saving to formats like JPEG or PNG discards any alpha channels you've created, permanently losing saved selections.

### Spot channel preservation

Spot channels have the most limited format support. Only photoeditor formats (PSD, PSB), TIFF, and PDF reliably preserve spot channels. DCS 2.0 format saves spot channels but discards alpha channels. Most other formats ignore spot channels entirely.

When working with spot colors for print production, verify with your print service provider which format they prefer. TIFF often provides the best compatibility while preserving all necessary channel information.


====================================================================================================

## 342. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/adjust-color/hdr-and-channel-manipulation/hdr-images-in-photoeditor.html

Understand how HDR images capture dynamic range beyond standard displays and how 32-bit format in professional photoeditor on desktop preserves real-world luminance.

Cameras can't capture the full range of light your eyes see in high-contrast scenes. You must choose between properly exposed highlights or detailed shadows. HDR images solve this by capturing and storing the complete luminance range, giving you extensive control over your final image.

## HDR images fundamentals

The visible world's dynamic range, from darkest shadows to brightest highlights, exceeds what cameras and displays can reproduce. Your eyes adapt continuously to different brightness levels, but traditional digital images compress this range into a fixed scale. You must sacrifice detail in either highlights or shadows when capturing high-contrast scenes.

HDR images remove this compromise. They store luminance values proportionate to actual light levels, preserving information across the entire brightness spectrum. Adjusting an HDR image's exposure works like changing camera settings during the original shoot, with the same flexibility to reveal hidden detail.

## photoeditor’s HDR storage format

photoeditor represents HDR images using 32 bits per channel with floating-point numeric values. Unlike standard images, this format stores actual light measurements.

In an HDR image, stored values correspond directly to light measurements in the photographed scene. A surface reflecting twice as much light receives a value twice as large. Because the relationship is proportional, you can make true exposure adjustments after capture, rather than just redistribute existing tonal values.

### 32-bit versus standard bit depths

Standard 8-bit and 16-bit images store values representing only the range from pure black to paper white. This narrow segment represents a small portion of real-world dynamic range. These formats encode display appearance rather than actual light levels.

The difference becomes critical when editing. With standard bit depths, you redistribute a fixed pool of tonal values. With 32-bit HDR, you access actual scene luminance data, allowing genuine exposure recovery rather than value stretching.

HDR images offer flexibility unavailable in standard formats because you're working with light information itself, not display values.

## HDR capture requirements

### Creating HDR images requires multiple exposures of the same scene at different brightness levels. Each exposure captures detail in different tonal ranges: one for highlights, one for midtones, one for shadows. When combined, these exposures provide the complete luminance information needed for a 32-bit HDR file.

The number of exposures depends on your scene's contrast range. High-contrast scenes with both bright sunlight and deep shadows need more exposures than scenes with moderate contrast. Each exposure must be identical except for brightness: any camera movement, focus changes, or lighting differences between shots creates problems during merging.

## HDR workflow integration

Understanding HDR concepts helps you make informed decisions about when and how to use this format. While 32-bit images preserve maximum flexibility, they require special handling since many photoeditor tools work only with 8-bit or 16-bit files.

HDR photography excels for high-contrast scenes where standard single-exposure photography fails: architectural interiors with windows, landscapes at sunrise or sunset, and any situation where you need detail in both bright and dark areas simultaneously.


====================================================================================================

## 343. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/apply-painting-techniques/fill-objects-selections-layers/painting-tools-overview.html

Learn about the different navigation and measuring tools you can access from the toolbar in professional photoeditor.

| **Tools** | **Description** |
| --- | --- |
| Brush tool | Paints brush strokes. |
| Pencil tool | Paints hard-edged strokes. |
| Color Replacement tool | Replaces a selected color with a new color. |
| Mixer Brush tool | Simulates realistic painting techniques such as blending canvas colors and varying paint wetness. |
| History Brush tool | Paints a copy of the selected state or snapshot into the current image window. |
| Art History Brush tool | Paints with stylized strokes that simulate the look of different paint styles, using a selected state or snapshot. |
| Gradient tools | Create straight-line, radial, angle, reflected, and diamond blends between colors. |
| Paint Bucket tool | Fills similarly colored areas with the foreground color. |
| Eyedropper tool | Samples colors in an image. |
| Color Sampler tool | Displays color values for up to four areas. |
| Clone Stamp tool | Paints with a sample of an image. |
| Pattern Stamp tool | Paints with part of an image as a pattern. |

## 344. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/apply-painting-techniques/fill-objects-selections-layers/fill-paint-bucket-tool.html

Learn how to fill areas of your image with solid colors or patterns using the Paint Bucket tool in professional photoeditor.

The Paint Bucket tool lets you quickly fill areas with similar colors to your chosen foreground color or pattern. It intelligently detects color boundaries based on your tolerance settings. Use it to fill the background, add color to shapes, or fill the selections in an image.

Select the Paint Bucket tool. If it isn’t visible, select and hold the Gradient tool  to access the hidden Paint Bucket tool.

Select a foreground or background color.

In the Options bar, select between the Foreground color or a Pattern. For Pattern, select one from the dropdown menu.

Under Blending, set the Mode for the fill.

Set the Opacity for the fill. Lower opacity values create more transparent fills.

Enter a Tolerance value (0-255). Lower values (10-20) fill similar colors, while higher values (100 or more) fill a broader range of colors.

Refine the fill behavior for your image:

*   Anti-alias: Create smoother edges on your filled areas.
*   Contiguous: Fill only connected areas, not the other parts of the image.
*   All Layers: Fill based on color data from all visible layers, not just the active one.

![Image 1](https://helpx-prod.scene7.com/is/image/HelpxProd/photoeditor-canvas-with-different-patterns-and-color?$pjpeg$&jpegSize=300&wid=1600)

Use the Paint Bucket tool to fill smooth edges with Anti-alias, limit fill to the selected area with Contiguous, or blend with All Layers. 

Select the area of the image to fill. The Paint Bucket fills all pixels within your specified tolerance range.

To avoid filling transparent areas, lock the layer's transparency in the Layers panel.

## 345. https://helpx.professional.com/photoeditor/desktop/apply-painting-techniques/fill-objects-selections-layers/stroke-selection-layer-color.html

Learn how to apply a colored stroke to a selection or layer in professional photoeditor to enhance visual impact or define edges.

Applying a stroke to a selection or layer creates a colored border around it. Strokes are useful for emphasizing elements or creating outlines. You can apply a stroke to any type of selection, including rectangular or elliptical shapes, freeform selections, or even text converted to a selection.

Select a foreground color.

Select the area you want to stroke using any selection tool. To stroke a layer, select it in the Layers panel.

Select Edit>Stroke.

If Edit>Stroke is grayed out, your layer might be a shape, text, or smart object. Right-click the layer and select Rasterize Layer from the shortcut menu.

In the Stroke dialog box, specify the width of the border in pixels.

Select Inside,Center, or Outside for Location to determine where the stroke appears over your selection or layer edge.

Under Blending, set the Mode and Opacity for the stroke. Lower opacity values create more transparent strokes.

Select Preserve Transparency to stroke only the areas of the layer that already have pixels.

Select OK to apply the stroke. A colored border appears around your selection or layer.

If the layer contents fill the entire image, a stroke applied outside the layer isn't visible.
